The Asian Productivity Organization (APO) 
is an intergovernmental organization that 
promotes productivity as a key enabler 
for socioeconomic development and 
organizational and enterprise growth. 
It promotes productivity improvement 
tools, techniques, and methodologies; 
supports the National Productivity 
Organizations of its members; conducts 
research on productivity trends; and 
disseminates productivity information, 
analyses, and data. The APO was established 
in 1961 and comprises 21 members.
APO Members
Bangladesh, Cambodia, ROC, Fiji, Hong 
Kong, India, Indonesia, Islamic Republic 
of Iran, Japan, ROK, Lao PDR, Malaysia, 
Mongolia, Nepal, Pakistan, Philippines, 
Singapore, Sri Lanka, Thailand, Turkiye, 
and Vietnam.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | V
W
e extend our deepest gratitude to God Almighty. By His grace, The
National Productivity Master Plan for Indonesia has been prepared as 
a strategic instrument to strengthen resilience and prosperity in the years 
ahead. Indonesia is entering a defining moment in its development trajectory. 
Our archipelagic strength, youthful demographic, and natural endowments 
meet a world reshaped by geoeconomic fragmentation, rapid advances in AI, 
climate volatility, and shifting trade rules. To ensure dignity and higher living 
standards in this setting, Indonesia must do more than grow. It must produce 
better, extracting greater value from knowledge, capital, and time across 
every region and sector.
Law No. 59 of 2024 on the National Long-Term Development Plan (RPJPN) 
2025–45 codifies the strategic pathway toward Indonesia Emas 2045. The 
master plan translates this mandate into sequenced, time-bound milestones 
that integrate national priorities with regional execution. Its design is directed 
to structurally close interregional development gaps, institutionalize higher 
standards of public service delivery, and ensure that the transformative 
momentum of national progress is equitably transmitted from growth centers 
to frontier and outermost regions.
Productivity is the foundation of this effort. The National Productivity 
Master Plan positions total factor productivity at the heart of the development 
approach, so that firms scale faster, technologies diffuse more widely, and 
services to citizens become quicker and more reliable. This is supported 
by integrated measures spanning skills and lifelong learning, R&D and 
innovation linkages, quality infrastructure and logistics, as well as capable 
public institutions that reduce frictions for people and businesses. 
As just one concrete example, this master plan emphasizes smart logistics and 
digital infrastructure, together with education and R&D. By improving ports 
and corridors with intelligent systems, strengthening data infrastructure, and 
deepening links among universities, research centers, and industry, Indonesia 
can reduce frictions, speed up the movement of goods and ideas, and raise 
value added across regions. These priorities are explicitly highlighted in the 
master plan’s strategic levers and implementation focus.
MSMEs are not merely beneficiaries; they are lead actors. Beyond access 
to finance and certification, the plan encourages value-chain partnerships 
that connect MSMEs with large enterprises, helping smaller firms adopt 
standards, technology, and managerial practices more rapidly so that local 
innovation becomes a durable source of quality jobs and regional resilience.
Delivery will determine credibility. This master plan is intended as a shared 
reference for ministries, agencies, provincial governments, the private sector, 
social partners, and universities and training centers as well as communities 
and civil society. It establishes guidance for program alignment, budgeting 
coherence, and result-oriented monitoring and evaluation so that policies 
reinforce each other and progress can be tracked transparently. We express 
our appreciation for the partnership of the Asian Productivity Organization 
and acknowledge the contributions of experts and stakeholders who have 
helped shape this document.
FOREWORD

VI | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
This National Productivity Master Plan is intended as an operational, 
collaborative framework that converts strategic commitments into institutional 
capabilities and capabilities into measurable outcomes, ensuring that by 2045 
Indonesia emerges as a high-income nation that is productive, equitable, and 
resilient to future challenges.
May the Almighty God continue to bestow His blessings upon our shared 
endeavor for a better Indonesia.
Jakarta, October 2025 
Rachmat Pambudy 
Minister for National Development Planning 
Head, National Development Planning Agency
FOREWORD


===== PAGE 9 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | VII
FOREWORD
Indonesia stands at an important juncture. Building on years of steady progress, 
the pathway to higher and more inclusive living standards will increasingly 
depend on raising productivity and extracting more value from each hour 
worked and each rupiah invested. This National Productivity Master Plan 
for Indonesia provides a coherent framework for translating that ambition 
into practical action and is aligned with national priorities and Indonesia’s 
long-term development vision.
The case for acting now is clear. As the economy continues to evolve, 
Indonesia faces both headwinds and opportunities: shifting global value 
chains, accelerating technological change, the imperative for greener and 
more resilient growth, and the need to ensure that the demographic transition 
yields broad-based dividends. Without a decisive shift toward productivity-
led growth, these dynamics could constrain the pace at which incomes rise 
and capabilities deepen across sectors, regions, and firm sizes.
The Master Plan meets this challenge by placing productivity, particularly 
total factor productivity, at the center of the development agenda. It advances 
integrated policies that strengthen the enabling environment for enterprise 
dynamism: regulatory coherence; investment in skills and lifelong learning; 
diffusion and adaptation of technologies; linkages between innovation 
and research and development (R&D); modern, intelligent infrastructure 
and logistics; and capable public institutions. These are nation-building 
investments whose benefits compound over time, especially for micro, small, 
and medium-sized enterprises and the communities they anchor.
At the heart of the Master Plan’s strategy is the 8-4-2-2 movement, which will 
become a unifying banner for collective action. It articulates an indicative 
growth narrative in which output expansion of around 8% is underpinned by 
contributions from labor participation and quality (4%), capital deepening 
(2%), and innovation (2%). This is not a rigid target but a mobilization device 
toward a shared productivity mission and a simple way to align programs, 
budgets, and accountability across ministries and provinces, the private 
sector and social partners, and universities and training centers.
Implementation discipline will determine outcomes. Indonesia already has a 
rich tapestry of productivity-relevant initiatives; the binding constraint is often 
fragmentation. The Master Plan therefore emphasizes whole-of-government 
and whole-of-society coordination, clear roles and mandates, predictable 
rules, and results-focused monitoring and evaluation. Its phased roadmap for 
2025–2029 comprising baseline and assessment, acceleration and integration, 
and scaling and sustainability aims to build momentum, secure early wins, 
and manage risks. Cross-cutting principles such as regional balance, decent 
work, gender equality, social inclusion, environmental sustainability, and 
data-informed decision-making are embedded to ensure that productivity 
gains translate into shared prosperity.
As a neutral, technical partner committed to evidence-based policy dialogue, 
capacity development, and peer learning across member economies, the 
Asian Productivity Organization is honored to support this agenda. As the 
Government of Indonesia’s lead ministry for the development of this Master 
Plan, the Ministry of National Development Planning/National Development 
Planning Agency (BAPPENAS) has provided overall stewardship by setting 
policy direction, orchestrating internal government coordination, and 


===== PAGE 10 =====

FOREWORD
convening partners throughout the drafting process. We also acknowledge 
the strong partnership of the national productivity organization of Indonesia, 
the Directorate of Productivity Development, Ministry of Manpower, whose 
operational expertise and mandates will be central to implementation; 
the relevant line ministries and agencies; the Indonesian Chamber of 
Commerce and Industry (KADIN) and private-sector associations; and other 
stakeholders whose contributions have shaped this document. Continued 
collaboration under BAPPENAS’s coordination will be essential for timely, 
results-oriented implementation.
With this Master Plan as a compass and the 8-4-2-2 movement as a rallying 
mechanism, Indonesia can convert today’s window of opportunity into 
durable, inclusive growth and advance confidently toward high-income 
status by 2045.
Tokyo, October 2025
Dr. Indra Pradana Singawinata 
Secretary-General, APO


===== PAGE 11 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | IX
Indonesia is the largest archipelagic state and the world’s 16th largest 
economy, which has sustained a steady 5% growth over the past two decades. 
With a population nearing 282 million and abundant young labor and natural 
resources, Indonesia aims to become an advanced economy by 2045 under 
the ambitious national development vision, Visi Indonesia Emas 2045. 
This vision includes reaching a per capita GDP of USD15,700 by 2038 and 
doubling current output to USD2.8 trillion.
However, productivity growth, a key driver of economic performance, remains a 
concern, averaging just 2.6% over the past decade, the lowest among major ASEAN 
nations. To address this, the nation’s leading economic planning body, Bappenas, 
has collaborated with the Asian Productivity Organization (APO) to launch 
the National Productivity Master Plan (MPPN). This master plan spans 2025–
2029 and aligns with the national development strategy, targeting productivity 
enhancement through evidence-based policies and strategic initiatives.
The dual structure of this master plan study encompasses in-depth analytical 
insights into Indonesia’s economic dynamics and a forward-looking strategy 
focused on achieving 8% annual growth by 2029, a critical step toward 
realizing the nation’s 2045 aspirations.
Beyond Growth: Sectoral Shifts and Structural 
Headwinds
Over the past two decades (2004–23), Indonesia’s economic growth has 
primarily been driven by private consumption and fixed capital formation, 
whereas the share of exports in GDP has steadily declined, currently ranking 
among the lowest in ASEAN (Figure 1).
Examining sectoral performance reveals an alarming contraction across all 
industrial sectors outside of services, particularly in manufacturing, which 
has long been regarded as the engine of structural transformation. Alongside 
rising employment in the service sector, this trend has intensified debates 
around Indonesia’s potential premature deindustrialization.

X | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Furthermore, the analysis highlights stark disparities at the regional 
and firm levels. The uneven distribution of key production factors 
has broadened output gaps between regions, suggesting persistent 
structural imbalances. Moreover, the production gap between large and 
medium enterprises versus micro and small firms remains substantial, 
underscoring 
entrenched 
capability 
divides 
within 
Indonesia’s 
industrial sectors.
Industrial Development and Growth Nexus
The manufacturing, agriculture, wholesale and retail trade, and mining 
sectors have driven Indonesia’s growth over the past two decades. Services, 
particularly information and communication as well as finance, have gained 
prominence, whereas sectors such as human health and social work have 
exhibited rapid growth from a low base.
Agriculture remains the largest employer; however, its job creation rate is 
slow, a trend mirrored by sluggish employment growth in the manufacturing 
sector. Conversely, employment in key service sectors has surged, reflecting 
a broader shift in the workforce toward services.
Meanwhile, manufacturing exports have weakened, dominated by low 
value-added and primary products, leaving the economy exposed to 
external trade volatility. Notably, Indonesia ranks lowest among ASEAN 
peers in high-tech and medium-high-tech exports, underscoring the 
urgent need for industrial upgrading and technological diversification 
(Figure 2).
Regionally, Java and Sumatra drive most of the national output, though 
manufacturing-led growth in Sulawesi and Maluku signals potential. 
Agriculture remains dominant in some remote provinces, whereas service 
industries are expanding nationwide, propelled by digitization and 
demographic changes.
While Indonesia’s evolving industrial landscape offers opportunities in 
emerging service sectors, the structural weaknesses in manufacturing, 
particularly the prevalence of small-scale, low-impact enterprises, call for 
revitalization to sustain inclusive and resilient growth.

===== PAGE 13 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | XI
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
TFP 
Matters: 
Unpacking 
Indonesia’s 
Growth 
Architecture
Indonesia’s long-term growth has historically been anchored in labor and 
capital accumulation; however, empirical analysis reveals a strong correlation 
between output and TFP, with a coefficient estimated at 0.89. This coefficient 
emphasizes the increasingly pivotal role of TFP in sustaining economic 
expansion, signaling that Indonesia stands at a critical juncture to transition 
from input-driven development to a TFP-led growth paradigm.
Certain service sectors such as information and communication and health and 
social work demonstrated robust TFP-led growth. In contrast, manufacturing, 
historically regarded as the engine of Indonesia’s structural transformation, 
has underperformed. TFP growth helped offset declines in labor and capital 
in specific industries; however, most sectors recorded negative TFP growth 
between 2013 and 2022.
Considerable regional disparities persist in productivity levels and industrial 
capacity. DKI Jakarta far outpaces other provinces in per-employee 
productivity, with regions such as Nusa Tenggara, Papua, and Maluku trailing 
significantly. The region of Java remains the country’s economic core; 
however, its TFP performance lags behind the national average, revealing 
a more complex growth dynamic. Conversely, Kalimantan and Papua have 
shown notable TFP gains. At the firm level, small and micro enterprises, 
representing 99.9% of all businesses, persistently exhibited negative TFP 
growth and severe output contraction. In contrast, large and medium-sized 
firms achieved modest growth, primarily driven by relative productivity 
advantages. This productivity dualism has significant implications for 
inclusive and scalable economic development.
International comparisons confirm the urgency of Indonesia’s productivity 
agenda. While Indonesia’s relative TFP (rTFP) has outperformed that of 
peers such as Malaysia and Vietnam, it continues to lag far behind advanced 
economies. For example, the United States reported an rTFP of 2.08 over the 
past three decades, compared with Indonesia’s 0.339. This contrast emphasizes 
the need to elevate TFP, especially among small and micro enterprises, as a 
central pillar of national competitiveness and long-term economic resilience.
In summation, the following insights distill the productivity narrative 
uncovered in the study:

XII | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
	•
TFP is central to future growth, with traditional inputs (labor and capital) 
offering diminishing returns.
	•
Manufacturing requires revitalization, as productivity has stagnated in this 
historically transformative sector.
	•
Service sector gains are uneven, with promising growth masked by variation 
in impact.
	•
Regional disparities and a wide enterprise capability gap underscore the 
need for spatially and institutionally targeted interventions.
	•
Innovation, skills, and institutional strength (not just capital) are pivotal to 
raising productivity.
	•
Despite outperforming some regional peers, Indonesia’s productivity still 
trails far behind global leaders.
The Strategic Foundation: Vision, Challenges, and 
Strategy
As stated earlier, this master plan is structured into two main parts. First, a 
diagnostic analysis of Indonesia’s productivity and growth performance, 
highlighting key structural challenges such as input-driven growth, lagging 
technological capacity, and regulatory fragmentation. Second, it articulates a 
national vision, mission, and strategy anchored in the aspiration of building a 
“Smart People, Smart Economy, and Smart Nation” (Figure 4). This framework 
positions productivity, particularly TFP, as the foundation of Indonesia’s long-
term transformation toward inclusive and competitive growth by 2045.
Strategic Levers and the Path Forward
To realize Indonesia’s productivity potential and position TFP as the engine 
of long-term growth, the Government of Indonesia should prioritize the 
following five strategic levers:
1.	 Build national consensus through productivity awareness campaigns and 
grassroots initiatives that reduce inefficiencies and postharvest losses.

===== PAGE 15 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | XIII
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
2.	 Reorient development around TFP, ensuring regulatory coherence and 
institutional support for innovation.
3.	 Modernize infrastructure and accelerate digital transformation, including 
smart logistics and regional techno-parks.
4.	 Strengthen education and research and development (R&D) linkages to 
equip the workforce with future-ready skills and commercialize innovation.
5.	 Diversify growth drivers and close the enterprise gap by upgrading lagging 
sectors and integrating micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises 
(MSMEs) into national value chains.
To translate these strategic levers into action, a phased roadmap, aligned 
with the 2025–2029 planning cycle, outlines the sequencing of the five key 
pillars and their enabling initiatives (Appendix 1). It progresses through the 
following three phases:
1.	 Baseline and Assessment;
2.	 Acceleration and Integration; and
3.	 Scaling and Sustainability.
Each stage aims to build momentum, secure outcomes, and mitigate risks 
(Appendix 2 presents further details).
Strategic implementation must move beyond design. Despite existing 
productivity-focused policies, their impact has been undermined by 
fragmented institutions and weak coordination; thus, achieving meaningful 
gains requires sustained, whole-of-government alignment.
The master plan’s five-year horizon is a vital window to embed a productivity-
driven trajectory. Determined and forward-thinking investment in 
technology, education, R&D, infrastructure, and institutions is a cornerstone 
of this repositioning of TFP, which is an essential catalyst for bridging the 
productivity gap and reaching inclusive, high-income growth by 2045.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 1
INTRODUCTION
Background
Indonesia is the largest archipelagic state worldwide, with over 17,000 islands within its national 
boundary. The land size is estimated at approximately 1,811,569 km², and the ocean area is 93,000 km², 
making it the 14th largest state globally in terms of territorial size.
Indonesia’s economy is one of the most vibrant economies worldwide. From 2003 to 2023, it maintained 
an average growth of approximately 5.0%, outperforming the global average of 3.51% over the same 
period (Figure 1).
In 2023, Indonesia emerged as the 16th largest economy worldwide on the nominal dollar term; it is, in fact, 
the eighth largest on the PPP basis.1 Indonesia is determined to uplift its economy to an advanced level, 
aiming to become one of the top five economies globally by 2045, the 100th anniversary of the nation’s 
independence. Such a vision for the nation’s development and prosperity is manifested in a roadmap 
for the long-term development strategy, widely known as the Visi Indonesia Emas 2045. Specifically, 
Indonesia envisions becoming a high-income country with a per capita GDP of approximately USD15,700 
by 2038 and doubling its current aggregated output to around USD2.8 trillion by 2045.
Indonesia is the fourth-largest country worldwide in terms of population size, which was estimated 
to be about 281.6 million in 2024.2 Furthermore, the majority of the young workforce represents a

INTRODUCTION
key national resource that bolsters the country’s long-term vision. In 2023, Indonesia’s workforce was 
estimated to reach approximately 189 million, or about 68.1% of the total population, increasing the 
feasibility of realizing such ambitious goals.3
Additionally, Indonesia also has abundant natural resources, particularly critical mineral resources, 
which are vital to advanced industries (i.e., ICT, electric vehicles, and renewable energy), including 
nickel (1.5 billion tonnes), copper (640 million tonnes), bauxite (927 million tonnes), and tin (1.2 billion 
tonnes).4 In 2021, about 69 tonnes of gold, 335 tonnes of silver, and 712 tonnes of copper were produced 
in Indonesia at the mine level. Indonesia’s nickel yield at the mine in 2023 was about 1.57 million 
tonnes, exceeding other producers and making Indonesia the largest producer worldwide.5
Agricultural products further constitute a necessary strategic resource. For example, Indonesia is the 
largest exporter of palm oil, accounting for 57% of global exports, or about 28 million tonnes.6 The 
country has sufficient resources to realize its vision. However, the key challenge lies in the optimal 
utilization of existing resources and their transformation into meaningful contributions to the aggregate 
output and national prosperity.
Nonetheless, Indonesia has been dormant in the middle-income country group for the last three 
decades despite its GDP surpassing USD1 trillion in nominal dollar terms since 2017. Indonesia must 
sustain an annual growth rate of at least 6% to attain its target per capita GDP of USD15,700 by 2038 
(Figure 2); however, its actual growth rate, having peaked at 7.4% in 2008, has never returned to 
the 6% level since 2013 (Figure 1) and has been hovering around the 5% level for the last 10 years, 
excluding the COVID-19 period. Although the nation’s recent growth has been stable and robust vis-
à-vis the world average, it remains insufficient to meet the objectives set forth in the Visi Indonesia 
Emas 2045. These goals risk becoming increasingly unattainable if growth continues to linger at the 
5% level.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 3
INTRODUCTION
Thus, in response to the president’s call for the 8% annual growth by 2029, the Government of Indonesia 
(GOI) is poised to pursue the acceleration of growth, as reflected in the Rencana Pembangunan Jangka 
Menengah Nasional 2025–2029 (RPJMN, Mid-term National Development Plan).7
The path to achieving the new growth target will not be an easy one. In its pursuit of higher growth, the 
GOI will be enmeshed in numerous obstacles, internal and external. Indeed, among the challenges, the 
stagnating, if not declining, productivity growth is the primary concern of policy-makers, academics, 
investors, and other stakeholders. Over the past decade, Indonesia’s average productivity growth has 
declined to 2.60%, the lowest among the major ASEAN members in the region (Figure 3). Therefore, 
the GOI must take urgent measures to reverse this trend and restore productivity growth to a sustained 
upward trajectory.
Objectives of the Development of the Master Plan for National 
Productivity
Based on the aforementioned backdrop, Bappenas, the national development planning ministry, in 
collaboration with the APO, aims to formulate a planning document that enunciates a basic policy 
framework to raise national productivity: The National Productivity Master Plan for Indonesia (Master 
Plan Produktivitas Nasional, MPPN).
This master plan spans five years, aligned with the RPJMN 2025–2029. This timeframe reflects the 
urgent need for agility and strategic foresight, as Indonesia’s economic landscape is rapidly evolving, 
driven by technological disruption and an increasingly volatile global politico-economic environment. 
Given these dynamics, the plan should be evaluated annually and undergo a comprehensive review to 
assess its validity and adequacy upon the completion of RPJMN 2025–2029.
Model of the Study
Growth, industrial development, and productivity are closely interconnected and intricately intertwined 
(Figure 4). Accordingly, this document aims to comprehensively examine and analyze mutual interactions 
within the industrial development-productivity-growth nexus. Furthermore, we seek to identify the 
driving forces behind national productivity and industrial growth, including potential bottlenecks that 
may hinder productivity and industrial development.
7	 Bappenas. (2025a). Narasi rencana pembangunan Jangka Menengah Nasional Tahun 2025-2029. Jakarta.

===== PAGE 20 =====

4 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INTRODUCTION
As such, this planning document focuses on assisting the GOI to identify key areas requiring prime 
attention, thereby effectively formulating strategies to address any hindrances while promoting areas 
where productivity and national competitiveness can be fostered.
Structure of the Study
This master plan is structured into two main parts. In line with the plan’s objective of supporting 
Bappenas’ efforts in evidence-based policymaking (EBPM), the first part (Chapters 2, 3, and 4) provides 
a comprehensive analytical foundation. The second (Chapter 5) part translates this into a forward-
looking strategy aimed at enhancing national productivity and advancing one of the core objectives 
of the RPJMN 2025–2029, achieving 8% economic growth by 2029. Chapter 6 concludes the study by 
summarizing the key findings and recommendations.
	•
Chapter 2 explores Indonesia’s economic growth and structural composition, examining the 
contributions of various industrial sectors at the national, regional, and firm levels.
	•
Chapter 3 evaluates how industrial sector growth affects national economic development. It analyzes 
sectoral output and workforce engagement across different scales and assesses international export 
competitiveness to provide a holistic understanding of the industry’s role in national progress.
	•
Chapter 4 investigates the relationship between productivity and national growth. It offers a detailed 
assessment of industrial capacity and productivity, particularly TFP, at the regional and firm levels, 
highlighting trends across provinces and broader geographic zones.
	•
Chapter 5 presents the vision, mission, strategic pillars, and strategic aims of the master plan. It also 
introduces a set of proposed strategic enablers and initiators, which are illustrative in nature, serving as 
conceptual recommendations rather than a catalog of existing policies. Ongoing programs already 
implemented by government agencies have been deliberately excluded from the delineated list of 
strategic enablers; however, some policies that may require recalibration to better align with their 
initially intended objectives are included as illustrative cases.
	•
Chapter 6 concludes the study with a concise synthesis of findings, reinforcing key insights and implications.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 5
ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Structure of Indonesia’s Growth
Indonesia’s economy, the largest in Southeast Asia, demonstrated remarkable resilience, rebounding to 
the prepandemic level of growth, approximately 5.30% in 2022 and 5.05% in 2023. As in the past, private 
consumption and investment have led Indonesia’s growth, which in 2023 accounted for approximately 
54.0% and 31.3% of the nation’s GDP, or IDR6.4 quadrillion and IDR3.9 quadrillion, respectively 
(Figure 1). Moreover, private consumption grew annually at about 4.34% in the last 20 years (2004–23), 
whereas investment grew at approximately 5.6%.8
Indonesia’s share of national investment has remained relatively stable in recent years compared with its ASEAN 
peers (Figure 2). It consistently hovered around the 30% ± 3% level, excluding the trough experienced during 
the post-Asian Financial Crisis period (1998 to 2007). Conversely, regional peers such as Malaysia and Thailand 
witnessed a sharp decline in their shares of investment, from 43.6% and 41.3% in 1995 to 20.4% and 21.7% in 
1999. Since then, neither Malaysia nor Thailand has regained their 30% level of investment recorded in 1998. 
Particularly, Malaysia’s investment share in GDP has dropped to less than 20% since 2021.9
Net exports regained their prepandemic momentum, growing significantly by 23.6% and 17.8% in 2022 
and 2023, respectively (Figure 3[a]). Between 2004 and 2023, the average annual growth rate of exports 
was approximately 6.2%, whereas imports grew at a rate of about 6.3%.

ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Nevertheless, Indonesia’s export contributions to GDP were low compared with its ASEAN peers. In 
general, it showed a downward trend, declining from about 34.1% in 2005 to about 21.7% in 2023. In 
the same year, the share exceeded 60% in Malaysia, Thailand, and Vietnam. Notably, Vietnam recorded 
approximately 87.2% of the export share in GDP (Figure 3[b]).
Meanwhile, inflation has been relatively well-managed and contained within the 3% range since 2016, 
excluding 2022, when it reached 4.2%. As such, it falls within a proximity of the target corridor of 2.5% 
± 1% for 2024 and 2025, as set forth by Bank Indonesia.10

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 7
ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
The spikes demonstrated in 2005–06, 2008, and 2014 reflect changes in GOI’s energy subsidy policies 
and the ensuing temporary consumer price hikes (Figure 1).11 Nevertheless, the surges were soon 
stabilized at a 4%–5% level and were managed at 1.92% in 2020 and 1.56% in 2021.
Industrial Sector Contributions to Growth
Like its ASEAN peers, Indonesia’s economy in the early days was dominated by the primary sector 
(agriculture, forestry, fishing, extractive activities, and the like) until the GOI initiated industrialization 
in the late 1960s. Since then, the manufacturing sector has steadily grown, ushering Indonesia’s economy 
to one of the most dynamic production centers in Southeast Asia.
Figure 4 demonstrates that in 1991, the manufacturing sector’s contribution to GDP, approximately 
20.1%, exceeded that of agriculture, forestry, and fishing, which stood at 19.7%, (the vertical line in the 
figure). Conversely, the share of manufacturing in GDP peaked at 32.0% in 2002, began to decline, and 
diminished to about 18.7% in 2023.
The agriculture, forestry, and fishing sector (hereinafter referred to as the “agriculture” or the “agricultural 
sector”) in Indonesia also experienced a significant decline. Once being responsible for nearly 40% of 
the nation’s output, the sector’s contribution continued to decline to about 12.5% in 2023.12
This structural change has also taken place in other ASEAN economies, such as Malaysia, Thailand, 
and Vietnam. For example, Vietnam’s agricultural contribution to GDP was as significant as 46.3%

ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
in 198613; however, it declined to 19.8% in 2005 and 12.0% in 2023. In 2023, agriculture’s contribution 
to GDP was 7.8% in Malaysia and approximately 8.6% in Thailand (Figure 5[b]).
In contrast, Vietnam’s share of manufacturing in GDP expanded rapidly from 18.8% in 2005 to 23.9% 
in 2023; however, the share declined in Malaysia, Thailand, and Indonesia (Figure 5[a]), with Indonesia 
experiencing the largest decline between 2005 and 2023. In fact, the share of manufacturing in GDP 
declined by 8.7%, from 27.4% in 2005 to 18.7% in 2023.
Conversely, the contribution of the industry sector excluding manufacturing in Indonesia was stabilized 
at a 20% ± 2% level since the early 2000s after two decades of decline from the peak of 31.3% in 1977. 
The contribution of the sector registered about 21.5% in 2023.
Contrary to the movement of other sectors, Indonesia’s service sector has consistently been expanding. 
The service sector’s contribution to GDP was merely 18.8% in the 1960s; however for the first time in 
Indonesia’s growth trajectory, it surpassed 40% in 2010, reaching approximately 40.7%, and continued 
onward. In 2023, the sector accounted for about 42.9% of GDP.14
Growth at the Regional Level
Indonesia consists of 38 provinces distributed across seven geographical regions, Sumatra, Java, Nusa 
Tenggara, Kalimantan, Sulawesi, Maluku, and Papua. Regional growth has varied considerably. Based 
on average growth rates from 2010 to 2023, Maluku recorded the highest growth at 7.33%, followed by 
Sulawesi (6.89%), Java (4.87%), and Sumatra (4.14%) (Figure 6).
In contrast, Nusa Tenggara, Papua, and Kalimantan experienced relatively low average growth rates 
during the same period, estimated at 3.83%, 3.79% and 3.62%, respectively.

However, it was Java that contributed the lion’s share to the national output, about 58.3%, or IDR5.6 
quadrillion (IDR5.6 × 1015), on average in the 2010–23 period, followed by Sumatra, about 21.8%, 
or IDR2.1 quadrillion (Table 1). The two regions, Sumatra and Java, accounted for nearly 80% of the 
nation’s average GRDP, or IDR7.7 quadrillion, for the 2010–23 period.
Besides the output, Java and Sumatra together make up about 77.5% of the nation’s population, or 
about 218 million. The population in Java alone accounts for about 55.7%, or 157 million. As such, 
the national wealth and critical production factors, capital and human resources, are amassed in certain 
regions, particularly in Java, whose average output is about 108 times greater than that of Maluku. 
Furthermore, population-wise, Java is approximately 48 times larger than Maluku.
As such, the distribution of national wealth and human capital is significantly skewed toward Java and 
Sumatra, becoming a source of uneven growth among the geographic regions in Indonesia.

ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Growth at the Firm Level
As of 2024, Indonesia has about 56,201,395 firms, of which 58,708 firms, or 0.1%, are large enterprises 
(LEs), whereas 56,142,687 firms, or 99.9%, are MSMEs.15 
However, the structure of output in the national economy is quite contrary to the distribution of firms by 
size.16 The average GVA generated by large and medium manufacturing enterprises during the 2012–22 
period was approximately 11 times greater than that of micro and small enterprises.17 Large and medium 
enterprises generated an average GVA of about IDR2.5 quadrillion during the same period; whereas, 
micro and small firms yielded about IDR0.22 quadrillion (Figure 6[a]).
With respect to growth rate, large and medium enterprises recorded an average growth of about 11.3% 
during the 2012–22 period on a CAGR basis, whereas micro and small firms registered about 5.9%. 
Notably, the growth propensity of large and medium-sized as well as micro and small-sized enterprises 
tends to decline over time. The growth rate of large and medium-sized enterprises declined from 27.9% 
in 2013 to about 7.4% in 2022. On the other hand, the negative growth of micro and small enterprises 
was significantly greater, plummeting from 44.3% in 2013 to 11.7% in 2022 (Figure 7[b]).
Chapter Recap
This chapter briefly explores Indonesia’s growth anatomy by analyzing data from the past two decades 
(2004–23). The analysis reveals that economic growth in the nation has been primarily driven by private 
consumption and gross fixed capital formation, viz., investment. While the GDP share of investment has 
relatively been steady, Indonesia’s share of exports, the lowest among its major ASEAN neighbors, has 
been showing a downward trend.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 11
ECONOMIC GROWTH IN INDONESIA
The analysis of growth data on Indonesia’s industrial sectors has yielded some important ramifications. 
First, besides the service sector, all other industrial sectors showed a decline in growth. Notably, the 
share of the manufacturing sector, the driver of the nation’s growth, has significantly contracted in the 
last 20 years. Coupled with the expansion of the service sector and a subsequent shift of workforce from 
manufacturing to services, this contraction has intensified debates (pros and cons) within academic and 
policy-makers’ circles over whether Indonesia was experiencing premature deindustrialization.18
Furthermore, the output gap among the regions is immense due to the lopsided distribution of critical 
production factors, indicating that a sharp disparity among the regions is perhaps inexorable.
Lastly, the output generated by large and medium enterprises substantially outweighs that of micro and 
small-sized firms. This situation indicates a significant disparity between medium and large firms and 
micro and small firms.
18	 See, for example, Andriyani, V. E. & Irawan, T. (2018). Identification of premature deindustrialization and its acceleration in Indonesia 
(period 1986-2015). Jurnal Ekonomi dan Kebijakan Pembangunan (Journal of Economics and Development Policy), 7(1), 78–101.

===== PAGE 30 =====

14 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Industrial Sector Output and Workers Engaged
As stated in the previous chapter, at the beginning of the 1990s, Indonesia experienced an upsurge in 
manufacturing, which had previously made only a modest contribution to the national economy. This 
marked the beginning of an economic transformation, with the manufacturing sector playing a pivotal 
role in driving the national economy from that point onward.
In the 2018–22 period, manufacturing accounted for about 20.2% of the nation’s aggregate output 
(Figure 1). Traditional industrial sectors such as agriculture, forestry and fishing, wholesale and retail 
trade, construction and mining and quarrying contributed about 13.5%, 13.5%, 10.8% and 9.2% to GDP, 
respectively. The mining and quarrying sector has recently reemerged as a primary industrial sector due 
to the upswing in international prices and demand for base metals.
In terms of average growth, the mining and quarrying sector attained the highest average annual growth 
in the period, about 18.7% on a CAGR basis (Table 1). Notably, non-traditional industries such as 
human health and social work activities (Q) and information and communication (J) reported relatively 
high growth rates of 10.6% and 9.8% respectively, whereas the growth of manufacturing was modest, at 
5.07%, during the 2018–22 period.

The analysis of industrial sectors from an employment perspective reveals the significance of agriculture, 
which remains the top sector that absorbs a majority of the nation’s workforce, 28.6%, followed by 
wholesale and retail trade and manufacturing, about 19.1% and 14.3%, respectively. 
It is also worth noting that over the past five years, employment growth in business (M, N), accommodation 
and food service (I), and human health and social work activities (Q) has been remarkably rapid. The 
business activities, transportation and storage, and human health and social work sectors experienced 
rapid employment growth of 7.35%, 5.46%, and 4.41%, respectively.
In contrast, the average annual workforce growth in manufacturing over the last five years was only 
0.8%. It is an indication that the role of the services sector in employment generation is expanding 
rapidly, whereas that of manufacturing is contracting.
Manufacturing Sector Output and Workers Engaged
According to the BPS industry classification, known as the Klasifikasi Baku Lapangan Usaha Indonesia 
(KBLI), 24 industries operate in the manufacturing sector. In Indonesia, the manufacturing sector plays 
a crucial role, leading the nation’s economic development and providing employment opportunities. The 
sector is also a hub of the nation’s innovation and technology development.
(continued from previous page)


===== PAGE 32 =====

16 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Of the industries in the sector, food and beverage account for the largest share, about IDR767,297 
or 23%, in terms of total output, followed by chemicals and articles of chemicals, 8.5%, motorized 
vehicles, trailers and semi-trailers, 7.0%, and basic metal, 6.5% (Table 2). Moreover, industries such as 
repair and installation for machines and equipment, products from coal and petroleum refinery, and base 
metals are expanding rapidly.
Nearly 47.5% of the manufacturing workforce is employed in the food and beverage and textile and 
apparel industries, highlighting their contribution to the national economy.
Notably, the growth of workers in the base metal and other manufacturing industries is relatively fast. 
Specifically, employment in the base metals industry grew at 7.9%, implying that a rapid development 
of the industry has been taking place over the last five years.

Export Competitiveness Assessment
Thus far, the analyses presented have portrayed Indonesia’s industry performance from a supply-side 
perspective. We must then consider the demand side. In particular, how do Indonesia’s industries fare in 
the global market demand?
In the 2000s, Indonesia’s export of goods and services expanded rapidly, accounting for about 34% 
of the nation’s GDP in 2005; however, it contracted to about 22% in 2023.19 As for the manufacturing 
sector performance, it accounted for approximately 22.5% of GDP in 2000 but diminished to merely 
8.4% in 2023. The growth rate of manufacturing exports once peaked at approximately 117% in 1979 
but experienced negative growth in 2023 (Figure 2).

===== PAGE 34 =====

18 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
The preceding assessment indicates that Indonesia’s exports may be losing their competitive edge in the 
global market, particularly in manufacturing. This situation is evident from the revealed comparative 
advantage (RCA) analysis of Indonesia’s 97 export products, which indicated that 37 items still retain 
international competitiveness (RCA > 1) (Table 3[a]). Nonetheless, most of Indonesia’s exports with a 
higher RCA fall into the category of primary and intermediate natural goods that do not require high-
tech inputs, resulting in low value added (Figure 4). This situation implies that Indonesia’s export base 
is relatively fragile and vulnerable to external shocks.
From the perspective of total export share, high-value-added merchandise groups, such as machinery, 
accounted for only about 14.1% of Indonesia’s exports. In contrast, the average for the eight OECD 
countries was about 47.5%. Notably, manufacturing powerhouses such as Germany, Japan, and Korea 
had machinery export shares of approximately 61.2%, on average, which is about four times higher 
than Indonesia’s (Table 3[b]). This amount is confirmed by an index that this study labels as the relative 
strength index (RSI) (Table 3[b]).
Indonesia’s RSI stands at 0.30, implying a relatively low level of technology exports is relatively low 
vis-à-vis the OECD 8. This figure suggests the fragility of Indonesia’s exports, despite is the country 
being on par with the average of the OECD 8 regarding the number of industries with international 
competitiveness, viz., RCA > 1.
Germany, France, and Italy have 50, 58, and 57 industries with international competitiveness, 
respectively, reflecting their highly stable export industry structures. Primarily, they possess a greater 
number of export industries with higher value-added and are less prone to external shocks.

Indonesia’s export fragility can be affirmed by specific statistical metrics derived from estimates of high-
value product exports, which are proxied by high-technology exports as a percentage of manufactured 
exports. In 2020, Indonesia recorded a share of about 8.4%, whereas Malaysia registered, by far, a 
higher growth rate of about 53.8%. Thailand and Vietnam reported high technology merchandise 
exports of about 27.7% and 42.7% respectively. Indonesia’s high technology exports were already low 
at 12.1% in 2010 and declined further to the 8% level over a ten-year period. In contrast, Vietnam was 
at a similar level to Indonesia in 2010 at approximately 13.1% but quickly rose to the 40% level over 
the same period.20
This trend is also observed in medium and high technology exports. Indonesia registered only 32% of 
manufactured exports as medium and high technology products compared with 63.2% in Malaysia, 
61.9% in Thailand, and 57.5% in Vietnam.21
20	 World Bank. (2025). World integrated trade solution WITS, Interactive Database. https://wits.worldbank.org/
21	 Ibid.


===== PAGE 36 =====

20 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
An important implication of this analysis is that Indonesia must diversify its export base toward a more 
technology-intensive and higher value-added structure in order to better insulate itself from export risks 
to some extent.
Development of Industries and Growth in the Regions
As stated in the preceding chapter, Indonesia’s growth is characterized by an extreme disparity. 
To recap, Java accounted for about 58.3% of the nation’s output on average, or IDR5.6 quadrillion 
(IDR5.6 × 1015), for the period 2010–23. Additionally, Maluku accounted for approximately 0.5%, or 
about IDR52 trillion.
The difference, in part, stems from the industry distribution that inevitably reflects the idiosyncrasies of 
local environmental factors, such as available resources, infrastructure, geography, consumer demand 
and needs, among others. For example, the mining and quarrying industry has been the primary driver 
of growth in Kalimantan and Papua, yielding, on average, about IDR281 trillion and IDR69 trillion, 
respectively, over the 2010–23 period.
Agriculture, often considered a lower value-adding industry compared with manufacturing, remains 
dominant in regions such as Sumatra, Sulawesi, Nusa Tenggara, and Maluku. Manufacturing, on the 
other hand, is the prevailing industry primarily in Java (Figure 4). Nonetheless, it is also a leading sector 
in regions such as Sumatra, Kalimantan, and Sulawesi, ranking second after agriculture.
In terms of growth, Maluku and Sulawesi reported the most significant growth over the last decade, 
as stated in the preceding chapter. Notably, manufacturing reported the highest annual growth in these 
regions, at approximately 18.52% in Maluku and 10.97% in Sulawesi, respectively (Table 4).
Analyzing the growth of the respective industries in all regions reveals certain interesting developments 
that have taken place in terms of industry structure. Table 4 shows that information and communication

The growth rate of the information and communication sector was also rapid in Kalimantan (8.47%), 
Sulawesi (9.27%), Maluku (7.06%), and Papua (6.46%). Overall, the growth rate of information and 
communication was high across all regions, suggesting its emergence as a leading industry driving 
Indonesia’s economic growth. This situation may reflect the implementation of the government’s policy 
to promote industrial digitalization and intelligentization.

Another prominent industry includes the human health and social work activities sector, which has 
exhibited a higher average annual growth compared with most other industries (Table 4). Again, this 
development was analogous across the regions. While manufacturing growth accounted for 4.43% in 
Java, the growth rate of human health and social work activities amounted to 7.73%. The growth rate 
of this service industry was about 6.54% in Sumatra, 6.15% in Nusa Tenggara, 8.60% in Kalimantan, 
7.91% in Sulawesi, 5.58% in Maluku, and 6.06% in Papua.
It is highly likely that the human health and social work activities industry will emerge as a leading 
industry in the upcoming decades, given the shifting population structure and the increasing proportion 


===== PAGE 38 =====

22 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
of older citizens (above 65 years of age). It accounted for 6.1%, or 15,101,298, in 2010 and rose to about 
8%, or 22,384,386, in 2024, representing an increase of approximately 48.2% over 14 years.22
Furthermore, the GOI prioritizes improving the quality of healthcare to position the sector for 
development using biotechnology, information technology, artificial intelligence, and similar 
technologies.23 Moreover, as the economy advances, individuals tend to become more health-conscious.
These developments are expected to drive increased demand, resulting in accelerated growth of the 
human health and social work activities sector.
Development of Industries and Growth at the Firm Level
As mentioned, approximately 4,343,000 firms operate in the manufacturing sector. Of these, large 
and medium-sized enterprises comprise about 30,300 firms, representing just 0.7% of the total. The 
remainder of the sector is predominantly composed of smaller businesses.
The food and beverage and textile industries, both vital contributors to Indonesia’s national economy, are 
characterized by smaller-scale operations. Specifically, approximately 97% of firms in these industries 
are classified as micro enterprises with only 0.5% falling under the large and medium-sized category. 
Similarly, the wood and cork products industry is overwhelmingly composed of micro-sized firms, 
constituting approximately 92% of the sector. In contrast, large and medium-sized enterprises represent 
a mere 0.2%, or 1,234 firms, of the industry (Table 5).
Among the 15.52 million workers employed in the manufacturing sector, approximately 46.7% 
(equivalent to 7.25 million individuals) are engaged in micro firms within the small-scale business 
category. An additional 14.5% (around 220,000 workers) are employed by small businesses. In contrast, 
large and medium enterprises account for 39.2%, representing 610,000 workers. These figures illustrate 
the diverse distribution of the labor force across firms of various sizes.
However, there a significant exception exists within specific industries. For example, in the electric 
and electronics industry sector as well as the automobile industry, over 95% of workers are employed 
by large and medium enterprises, illustrating a unique concentration of labor within these domains 
(Table 5).

Regarding industries’ contributions to Indonesia’s economic growth, the lion’s share of GVA generated 
by the manufacturing sector (approximately 93% or IDR301,471 billion) was attributable to large and 
medium enterprises. In contrast, small firms contributed a marginal 1.8%, equivalent to IDR58,146 
billion, whereas micro firms accounted for 5.8%, or IDR163,602 billion.24 These figures emphasize the 
pronounced disparities in output across firms of varying scales.
Furthermore, within industries that demand advanced technological capabilities and intensive R&D, 
large and medium enterprises accounted for over 99% of the GVA.25 Examples of such technology-
intensive industries include chemicals and chemical products, pharmaceuticals, chemical medicinal 
goods, computers, electronic and optical products, electrical equipment, motorized vehicles, and trailers 
and semi-trailers. This stark disparity evidences the considerable technological divide between large-
scale enterprises and smaller firms.
Chapter Recap
The association of industrial development and growth was analyzed in this chapter, which disclosed 
some significant aspects of Indonesia’s industrial development-growth nexus. Indonesia’s growth was 
primarily driven by manufacturing, agriculture, wholesale and retail trade, and mining and quarrying. 
24	 Authors’ estimate based on BPS data (2024); see also the Section 3-2 in Chapter 3 of this document.

===== PAGE 40 =====

24 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
It is important to note that information and communication as well as financial and insurance activities 
further contribute significantly to the nation’s growth. Despite their relatively small contribution to 
overall growth, the human health and social work activities sector has exhibited rapid expansion.
Undoubtedly, agriculture remains the largest sector, absorbing the majority of the nation’s workforce. 
Nevertheless, employment growth in this sector has been relatively slow. A similar pattern is observed 
in manufacturing, where employment growth was also sluggish during the 2018–22 period.
In contrast, service sector industries such as business activities, transportation and storage, and human 
health and social work sectors experienced rapid employment growth. This trend suggests a shift in 
workforce migration, favoring industries within the service sector.
On the other hand, Indonesia’s exports of goods and services over the last two decades have experienced a 
downturn. The analysis revealed that Indonesia’s manufactured exports predominantly consist of goods with low 
value-added primary and intermediate products, underscoring the fragility of Indonesia’s manufacturing exports.
Some statistical metrics disclosed that Indonesia’s high-technology product exports, along with its 
medium- and high-technology product exports, are by far the lowest among its ASEAN peers. Thus, 
Indonesia must diversify the export industry structure into a more technology-imbued, higher added-
value one in order to insulate itself from export risks to some extent.
This study also examined the fabric of growth and industry distributions across regions. In other words, 
Indonesia’s growth was characterized by poignant disparity among regions, with Java and Sumatra 
leading by a large margin compared to other regions. Nevertheless, the growth rate was high in regions 
such as Maluku and Sulawesi, primarily driven by the manufacturing sector.
However, agriculture remains the predominant industry in Sumatra, Sulawesi, Nusa Tenggara, and 
Maluku. Manufacturing, by contrast, is the prevailing industry only in Java. Nevertheless, it remains a 
leading industry in Sumatra, Kalimantan, and Sulawesi.
Over the past decade, notable developments have emerged in Indonesia’s industry horizon, particularly 
the rise of service industries such as the information and communication as well as the human health 
and social work activities sectors. These shifts can be attributed to changes in environmental factors, 
including digitalization, intelligentization, and demographic transitions.
While employment growth in the manufacturing sector has remained sluggish, specific service sector 
industries, such as business activities, transportation and storage, and human health and social work, 
have experienced rapid employment growth. This trend indicates a significant shift in workforce 
migration, favoring the service sector industries.
The implications of these developments are certainly noteworthy. On the one hand, they present an 
opportunity for the Indonesian industry to expand into new growth sectors. On the other hand, they 
underscore the urgency of revitalizing the manufacturing sector at the national and regional levels.
Despite these prospects, the manufacturing sector faces substantial challenges, many of which emerge from 
structural constraints. One such challenge includes the disproportionately large size of the small-scale industry 
sector within manufacturing, which contributes less to the national economy than expected given its scale.

PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Indonesia’s Growth Paradigm
Indonesia’s growth over the past 50 years can be characterized as a period of factor-input- 
driven development with growth primarily achieved through labor and capital inputs. The combined 
share of contribution of labor and capital to output in the period 1971–2022 reached about 98.4%. 
In contrast, the share of TFP, representing economic efficiency, was only 1.6% during the same 
period (Table 1).

Source: Authors’ estimate based on APO data (2024).
This observation highlights a key challenge in Indonesia’s growth trajectory: the economy has thus 
far relied heavily on factor inputs while falling short in their efficient utilization. The impact of this 
over-dependence on factor inputs are reflected in the trajectory of growth components. Indeed, the 
respective growth components exhibit a declining propensity. The output growth fell from 8.28% during 
1971–75 to 3.59% during 2016–22. Furthermore, labor productivity growth declined from 2.23% to 
1.13% during the same period, whereas capital deepening descended from 4.08% to 2.80%. TFP growth 
further plummeted from 1.96% to -0.35%.
Furthermore, this warrants special attention to the interplay between the output–TFP growth 
nexus. As shown in Figure 1, the growth of output and TFP appear to move in tandem. When 
Indonesia experienced overwhelming economic, social and/or natural shocks, the growth of output 
and TFP fell simultaneously. Notable examples include the volcanic eruption in West Java in 1982, 
Asian Financial Crisis in 1997, and COVID-19 Crisis in 2020. These events caused substantial socio-
economic damages, severely impairing normal economic activities and leading to sharp declines in 
output and TFP growth.
2–22
200


===== PAGE 43 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 27
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Concerning the 1982 West Java disaster, had an early warning system been active to mitigate the 
negative effects of natural disaster-related damage on TFP growth by, for example, 50%, Indonesia’s 
annual growth in 1982 would have approximately been 3.6% instead of 1.03%.26
In sum, it can be deduced that Indonesia’s growth is closely associated with TFP. Indeed, the association 
between growth and TFP is estimated at 0.89. Moreover, Indonesia should focus on shifting its growth 
paradigm from factor input-driven development to TFP-led growth.
As an OECD paper puts a simple but adequate definition of TFP, which is the “ability to ‘produce more 
with less,’”27 Indonesia would need to reassess its production practices by incorporating technology and 
innovation. Indeed, amidst growing global resource constraints and the pursuit of achieving the Green 
Growth goals, a TFP-led growth model would undoubtedly be a pertinent approach.
Industrial Capacity and Productivity
The analyses of the growth components of the respective industrial sectors in Indonesia are presented in 
Table 2 below. The analyses covered a 10-year period, from 2013 to 2022, unless specified otherwise. 
It is important to note that all productivity estimates were primarily based on the Solow model. If 
alternative models were used for certain estimates, this will be clearly indicated.
Growth Components of Manufacturing
As shown in the table, the industrial capacity of manufacturing, the primary driver of Indonesia’s 
economic growth, was sluggish during the 10-year period from 2013 to2022. Indeed, the average growth 
declined from 4.29% in the first half of the period to just 2.61% in the second half.
The growth of labor and capital inputs during the period declined from 1.09% and 4.95% in the first half 
to 0.73% and 2.55% in the latter half, respectively. However, TFP increased from -1.76% to -0.68%, 
registering a 1.08% rise. This increase, in fact, offset the declines in factor inputs, namely labor and 
capital, allowing manufacturing output in the second to reach 2.61%. Had it not been for the TFP 
increase, the manufacturing growth in the second half would have only amounted to1.53%.

This affirms a critical role of TFP in driving growth. Indeed, the growth of TFP during the period 
2013–22 was -1.22%, resulting in a negative impact of 35%, viz., -35%, on manufacturing output.

Growth Components of Transportation and Storage
The performance of transportation and storage, which likely has a significant effect on TFP growth, was 
also sluggish during the 2013–22 period, yielding an average annual growth of 5.37%.
The sector experienced a substantial decline in capital input, from 4.81% to -0.15%, in the second 
half of the period. However, the loss was compensated by a significant increase in TFP, from 1.72% to 
2.26%. With respect to growth in the transportation and storage sector, about 37% was attributable to 
the performance of TFP during the 2013-22 period.
Growth Components of Information and Communication
The information and communication sector demonstrated the highest growth among all industrial sectors 
during the period, achieving an average rate of 8.64%. While the growth of labor and capital slowed 
somewhat in the latter half of the period, TFP showed a significant increase, rising from 0.14% to 1.84%

30 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Approximately, 90% of the information and communication sector’s growth was driven by labor and 
capital inputs in the period 2013-2022, whereas the remaining 10% was attributed to TFP. Like other 
industrial sectors presented in the preceding sections, the information and communication sector is 
anticipated to make a substantial contribution to the nation’s TFP growth.
Growth Components of Education and Human Health Social Work Activities
During the period of analysis, labor and capital inputs accounted for approximately 94% of the output 
growth in the education sector. This was driven by labor growth of 0.86% and capital growth of 3.04%. 
TFP growth only accounted for 0.26%, contributing around 6% to the sector’s output.
On the other hand, TFP growth in the human health and social work sector stood at around 0.12%, which 
is lower than that of the education sector. This highlights the need for greater attention to both sectors, 
given their increasing importance in supporting the nation’s long-term economic development
Industrial Capacity and Productivity at the Regional Level
To assess the variation of industrial capacity and productivity across the 34 provinces in Indonesia, 
this study estimates per employee productivity and relative productivity of each province, dubbed 
as the provincial productivity per employee (PPE) and provincial relative productivity (PRP), 
respectively.28
A caveat: Indonesia officially comprises 38 provinces, following the division of Papua and West Papua, 
which engendered the formation of Papua Selatan (South Papua), Papua Tengah (Central Papua), Papua 
Pegunungan (Mountains Papua) and Papua Barat Daya (Southwest Papua).
However, as these newly established provinces have yet to accumulate sufficient data, they are 
excluded from provincial-level analyses. Consequently, when this document refers to “all provinces,” it 
specifically refers to the 34 provinces included in the analysis.

Industrial Capacity and Productivity by Province
DKI Jakarta recorded the highest PPE, amounting to IDR384.6 million per employee, whereas Nusa 
Tenggara Timur registered the lowest, at IDR25.4 million. This demonstrated a striking variation, with 
the highest PPE being 15.1 times greater than the lowest (Table 3).
In the case of the PRP, DKI Jakarta was the highest (9.06); whereas, Kepulauan Bangka Belitung was 
the province with the lowest PRP (0.08). Thus, the difference between DKI Jakarta and Kepulauan 
Bangka Belitung was greater than 100 times.

Industrial Capacity and Productivity by Region
The regional productivity per employee (RPE) and the relative regional productivity (RRP) of Indonesia’s 
seven regions are presented in Table 4 below. Kalimantan was identified as the region with the highest 
RPE, amounting IDR116.2 million, followed by Papua with IDR94.0 million and Java with IDR88.8 
million per employee. Nusa Tenggara registered the lowest RPE, as shown in the indexed RPE, in which 
Kalimantan was assigned 100 as it was the region with the highest RPE. As such, the difference in RPE 
amounted to almost 3 times between the highest and the lowest region.
As with RRP, Java recorded the highest score (11.99), followed by Sumatra (3.12) and Kalimantan 
(2.36). Maluku was assessed to be the lowest performing region in RRP, (0.27).
As such, it can be concluded that Kalimantan is the region with the highest per-employee 
productivity, whereas Java exhibits the highest overall regional productivity. Approximately 80% 
of industrial capacity is concentrated in Java and Sumatra, particularly in Java, highlighting a stark 
regional disparity.
Indeed, apart from the agriculture, mining, and quarrying sectors, the majority of Indonesia’s industrial 
capacity is concentrated in Java, followed by Sumatra, though with a considerable margin of variation 
(Industrial sector GVA in Table 4). These variations reaffirm that acute regional disparities constitute 
an urgent productivity and growth challenge for Indonesia that must be addressed immediately. Such 
disparities, needless to say, hinder equitable resource distribution and exacerbate socio-economic 
inequalities across regions.

Source of Growth and TFP at the Regional Level
Source of Growth and TFP at the Provincial Level: Selected Provinces
TFP and Growth in DKI Jakarta: As stated in the preceding section, DKI Jakarta has the highest 
production capacity in Indonesia. However, its average output growth of 3.83% during the period 2013–
22 was relatively lower than its capital growth of 5.90% during the same period (Table 5: the Solow 
Model estimate). This discrepancy is attributable to the negative impact of TFP growth, measured at 
-2.17%. This suggests that the GOI needs to investigate the contributing factors of TFP growth and the 
efficiency of capital input in the area.
TFP and Growth in the Province of Bali: An examination of Bali’s productivity dynamics 
revealed a noteworthy interplay between growth and TFP. Specifically, the RPE and PRP 
indices for Bali were observed to be at a relatively lower level. Although the growth rate of 
labor input in Bali remained marginal, the expansion of capital input was markedly significant. 
Nevertheless, despite this substantial capital allocation, the recorded output growth was limited 
to 3.21%, indicating that the marginal contribution of capital input failed to meet the anticipated 
output growth.
The discrepancy could be attributed to the average TFP growth, which was estimated at -6.94%, 
effectively offsetting the positive effect of capital input. In other words, the analysis suggested that the 
efficiency of capital input in Bali was so low that it offset the province’s overall growth rate.
TFP and Growth in the Province of Kalimantan Timur: As estimated at 74 for the PPE index and 21 
for the PRP, the Province of Kalimantan Timur was assessed to be at a high level. The analysis of the 
growth and productivity of Kalimantan Timur revealed that it was characterized by a highly balanced 
distribution of growth components. The contributions of labor input, capital input, and TFP were 
(continued from previous page)


===== PAGE 50 =====

34 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
estimated to be 36%, 26%, and 38%, respectively, indicating that the province was equipped with a 
stable growth system (See the column, “2013–22 Share, %” in Table 5,).
TFP and Growth in the Province of Papua Barat: An analysis of productivity in the Province of Papua 
Barat revealed a notable insight regarding TFP. Despite substantial labor and capital inputs between 
2013 and 2022, together accounting for approximately 96% of growth, TFP contributed only 4%, with 
an average periodic growth rate of just 0.12% (See Table 5). As a result, the province’s overall growth 
was limited to 3.27%.
The preceding findings and accompanying discussion underscore an important message: TFP growth 
is likely influenced by tangible inputs such as labor and capital as well as intangible “software” factors 
such as governance quality, institutional capacity, and social capital, as exemplified by the case of Bali.
Therefore, further inquiry into the underlying drivers of TFP growth is necessary, with particular 
attention to the role of these non-material inputs in shaping long-term productivity dynamics.
Comparison of the TFP Growth across Provinces: To facilitate a meaningful comparison of TFP growth 
across provinces, while accounting for their unique environmental conditions that may not be captured 
in the Solow model, this study examines the “absolute TFP growth level” of each province, which will 
be denoted as “aTFP”29 in this study.
Figure 3 illustrates the average TFP growth levels of all provinces during the period 2013–22. 
Kalimantan Timur recorded the highest TFP growth, with an annual rate approximately 2.8 times the 
national provincial average, followed by Kepulauan Riau (2.6), Kalimantan Utara (about 2.5), DKI 
Jakarta (2.3), and Papua Barat (2.1).

Source of Growth and TFP at the Regional Level
TFP and Growth in Java: As outlined in the preceding sections, Java remains the region with the nation’s 
leading production capacity. However, the average output growth of 3.58% during the period 2013–22 
falls short of expectations, particularly in light of considerable factor inputs, particularly the 4.46% 
capital input (Table 6).
The observed shortfall in output growth can be attributed to the contraction in TFP, which declined by 
-1.54%, thereby exerting a significant adverse impact on overall output performance (Table 6). This 
phenomenon parallels the case of DKI Jakarta, as elaborated in the preceding section. Accordingly, the 
GOI to prioritize measures that ensure the efficient allocation and utilization of capital investments to 
foster sustainable growth.
TFP and Growth in Nusa Tenggara: The growth patterns in Nusa Tenggara were similar to those of 
the Province of Bali, as outlined in the preceding section. Specifically, labor growth was relatively 
modest, whereas capital growth reached a significant 9.59%. However, output growth remained 
constrained at 3.32%, indicating a relatively limited impact of capital investment. This disparity 
suggests that capital inputs did not yield substantial positive outcomes for Nusa Tenggara’s 
economic performance.
This situation can be attributed to the contraction in TFP, which declined by -7.16%, effectively negating 
the impact of capital inputs. In other words, the efficiency of capital utilization in Nusa Tenggara was 
negligible, thereby impeding the overall economic growth of Nusa Tenggara.

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 37
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
TFP and Growth in Papua: The RPE and RRP indices for Papua were estimated at 81 and 5, respectively, 
positioning the region’s RPE within the upper tier while RRP remained in the lower echelon. The 
patterns of productivity and output growth are similar to those observed in the Province of Papua 
Barat. Specifically, while labor and capital inputs across the entire Papua region were relatively 
modest in absolute magnitude, they exhibited a sustained upward propensity. The contribution 
of TFP to growth (6%) was moderate yet essential in underpinning Papua’s stable and sustainable 
growth dynamic.
Comparison of TFP Growth across the Regions: Based on absolute TFP levels (aTFP), the comparative 
analysis of the seven regions in Indonesia yielded significant insights into TFP growth.
Java demonstrated commendable production capacity; however, it is important to underscore that 
the level TFP (aTFP) is estimated at 0.09, slightly below the national average of 0.1 (Table 8). This 
estimation implied disproportionate reliance on factor inputs to sustain growth momentum, posing 
productivity challenges to Java’s economic dynamism.
Comparison of TFP Growth across the Regions: Based on absolute TFP levels (aTFP), the comparative 
analysis of the seven regions in Indonesia yielded significant insights into TFP growth.
Java demonstrated commendable production capacity; however, it is important to underscore that 
the level TFP (aTFP) is estimated at 0.09, slightly below the national average of 0.1 (Table 8). This 
estimation implied disproportionate reliance on factor inputs to sustain growth momentum, posing 
productivity challenges to Java’s economic dynamism.
Papua and Kalimantan led the growth of the nation’s TFP, whereas Nusa Tenggara by far lagged behind 
other provinces. The outcomes reaffirmed the results of the source of growth presented in Table 6 in the 
preceding section. That is, physical inputs did not merely drive the TFP growth but also reflected the 
influence of other intangible factors.
In addition, the case of Nusa Tenggara implied that a high level of factor input, particularly capital input, 
did not directly translate into growth. Accordingly, the GOI and regional authorities should reexamine 
their policies to assess whether they are effectively designed to enhance and optimize resource allocation 
as well as subsequent performance.
Source of Growth and TFP at the Firm Level
As stated in the preceding section, this study has used the Solow model for basic productivity estimation. 
Although the model serves as a cornerstone of modern economics by providing valuable insights into 
the structure of growth components, it also has several limitations, particularly when used to compare

===== PAGE 54 =====

38 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
entities with unique characteristics, i.e., the characteristics of economic structures (e.g., technology 
levels, logistics, and market size).30
One concerning issue is that a simple, direct comparison of productivity across diverse forms 
of various sizes in Indonesia, based on the Solow model, may not be ideal, particularly when 
estimating TFP. The resulting productivity of the “Solow residual” does not account for the unique 
conditions and characteristics of firms’ production processes and conditions, including the size of 
input factors.
Therefore, this study adopts the estimation of relative levels of TFP (henceforth referred to as the 
“Relative Level Model”)31 to compare TFP growth among different regions when necessary.32
The manufacturing sector has long served as the primary driver of Indonesia’s economic growth. 
However, during the second half of the 2013–22 period, output growth slowed significantly, 
declining to 2.61% from 4.29% in the first half of the period despite capital growth contributing 
approximately 149% to total output growth. This deceleration can be attributed to negative 
TFP growth, estimated at -2.68%, which accounted for a -78% contribution to overall output 
growth (Table 7).
This trend was even more pronounced in the small business sector, where the periodic GVA declined 
at an annual average rate of –9.88%. This contraction can be attributed to the negative TFP growth, 
estimated at –5.26%, which significantly contributed to the sharp decline in output growth within 
the sector.
Unlike small businesses, large and medium-sized firms experienced an annual growth rate of 3.86% 
during the 2013–22 period; however, negative TFP growth, estimated at –3.46%, appears to have 
significantly dampened overall output growth, contributing to -90% (Table 7).
In contrast, micro enterprises exhibited periodic output growth of 4.24%, which was adversely impacted 
by the negative TFP growth of –3.13%. These findings highlight the critical role of TFP in shaping 
growth dynamics across different sizes of business activities.
An analysis of TFP dynamics for large and medium-sized, small, and micro firms during the 2013–22 
period, based on the relative level model, reveals several noteworthy insights (Table 8). The relative 
TFP (rTFP) of large and medium-sized firms exceeded the benchmark average of 1 across all firm 
sizes, reaching 1.458. In contrast, the rTFP for small and micro firms was estimated at 0.435 and 0.403, 
respectively. This outcome suggests that, excluding large and medium-sized firms, the TFP of the 
manufacturing sector exerted a negative influence on growth, effectively offsetting the contributions of 
labor and capital inputs.
The policy implications that can be drawn from these exercises are clear: urgent and targeted 
measures to enhance TFP for small and micro firms must be prioritized. This necessity arises from 
the fact that these firms constitute 99.9% of all enterprises, highlighting their critical role in the 
national economy.
