PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Indonesia’s Productivity and Growth in the International Setting
As of 2023, trade accounts for approximately 41% of Indonesia’s GDP, highlighting its critical role in 
the national economy. Key trading partners include China, the United States, Japan, India, Malaysia, and 
Korea.33 Notably, Indonesia’s trade has shown an upward trend in recent years, highlighting its increasing 
integration into the global market. This upward propensity presents an important question: How does 
Indonesia’s productivity (in other words, competitiveness) compare with its major trading partners?
A comparative analysis of the growth components across countries may offer invaluable insights, 
particularly regarding the impact of productivity on economic growth. To this end, this study employed 
a 30-year dataset (1991–2019) to estimate how average productivity growth influences the respective 
countries’ economic performance (Figure 5).
Figure 5(a) presents the Solow model estimate, showing that China achieved an impressive average 
annual growth rate of 6.25% over the past 30 years. Among the contributing factors, capital growth 
(4.53%) accounted for approximately 73% of the total growth, whereas labor growth (0.43%) contributed 
7%. Notably, TFP exhibited an annual growth rate of 1.29%, accounting for approximately 21% of 
China’s remarkable economic expansion.
As Japan has transitioned into one of the world’s advanced economies, its economic system has 
experienced a decline in efficiency, evidenced by the negative growth it has sustained over the past 
30 years.
In the case of Indonesia, analyzing the average productivity components of the six selected countries 
reveals key insights. The contribution of TFP to national growth was only 10% for Indonesia, significantly 
below the average of 16% estimated across the selected countries.
The relative level model estimation, however, disclosed some contrasting insights. In the case of Japan, 
while the growth rate of TFP and output under the Solow model estimation was negative, the relative 
level model analysis indicated that Japan’s relative TFP (rTFP = 2.362) was approximately 2.4 times 
higher than the average of other countries. This finding emphasizes that Japan remains a formidable 
manufacturing powerhouse.34
Among the countries analyzed, the relative TFPs of China, Japan, and Korea were estimated to exceed 
the average (1), indicating a higher-than-average productivity level. In contrast, Malaysia, Thailand, 
Vietnam, and Indonesia each had a relative TFP below 1, suggesting relatively lower productivity levels 
(Figure 5[b]).

===== PAGE 57 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 41
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Notably, Indonesia’s relative TFP (rTFP) was 0.818, surpassing Malaysia (0.569), Thailand (0.717), 
and Vietnam (0.22), indicating comparatively higher TFP. However, approximately half of Indonesia’s 
average growth over the last 30 years was attributable to TFP growth.35 This situation highlights the 
necessity for the GOI to recognize and prioritize the crucial role of TFP in driving sustainable economic 
growth and competitiveness.
The relationship between TFP and growth is re-examined through a comparative analysis of productivity 
in advanced economies. The outcomes of the analyses disclose that economies with excellent economic 
performance tend to possess a higher magnitude of TFP.
Over the past 30 years, the average TFP growth of five advanced economies (Canada, Germany, France, 
the United Kingdom, and the United States) was approximately 24%, whereas Indonesia’s TFP growth 
was merely 10% (Figure 6(a)). This stark disparity underscores Indonesia’s relative inefficiency in 
contributing to economic growth compared with advanced economies.
The rTFP of the U.S. was particularly significant, which was estimated at 2.08, far surpassing other 
economies in the comparison and highlighting its exceptional productivity levels (Figure 6[b]). While 
the rTFP of the U.S. exceeded 1, reflecting superior efficiency, the relative TFP of other economies 
(Canada, Germany, France, the United Kingdom, and Indonesia) was below 1. Specifically, Indonesia’s 
rTFP stood at 0.339, signaling a significant lag in productivity growth. These findings further emphasize 
the need for Indonesia to prioritize TFP growth as a matter of national urgency, given its critical role in 
enhancing economic efficiency and ensuring sustainable growth.
Chapter Recap
This chapter investigated Indonesia’s sources of economic growth by analyzing datasets from various 
periods, which has yielded some significant implications for understanding the nation’s growth structure. 
Over the past 50 years, Indonesia’s economic expansion has predominantly been driven by factor inputs, 
with growth primarily attributable to labor and capital contributions.
Nevertheless, a compelling observation emerged: output growth was closely aligned with the trajectory 
of TFP growth. This finding underscores the significance of the output–TFP nexus, particularly 
in the Indonesian context, where the correlation between these two elements was estimated at 0.89. 
Furthermore, given this relationship, it will be critical for Indonesia to shift its growth paradigm from a 
factor-input-driven growth model to one led by TFP.

42 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Analyzing the relationship between output and productivity across various industrial sectors also 
yielded significant findings. Specifically, service-oriented industrial sectors, such as information and 
communication as well as human health and social work activities, exhibited a relatively high annual 
growth rate exceeding 7%, driven by positive growth in labor, capital, and TFP. 
In contrast, the industrial capacity of manufacturing, the foundation of Indonesia’s growth, was sluggish 
over the past decade (2013–22), primarily due to a decline in labor and capital inputs; however, the 
growth of TFP offset this decline, affirming its critical role in economic expansion. Nonetheless, most 
industrial sectors (excluding a few such as electricity and gas, transportation and storage, information and 
communication, financial and insurance activities, education, and health and social work) experienced 
TFP declines.
This study also assessed the variation of industrial capacity and productivity across 34 provinces and 7 
regions in Indonesia by estimating per-employee productivity and relative productivity of each province 
and region (PPE, PRP, RPE, and RRP).36 The results revealed that DKI Jakarta exhibited the highest 
PPE level, whereas Nusa Tenggara Timur registered the lowest. Moreover, the disparity in employee 
productivity across provinces was considerable, as DKI Jakarta (indexed at 100) exhibited productivity 
over 14 times greater than Nusa Tenggara (indexed at 7).
The gap expanded significantly when production capacity was analyzed through PRP. The estimated 
differences between DKI Jakarta (100) and Kepulauan Bangka Belitung, Gorontalo, Sulawesi 
Barat, Maluku Utara, and Papua Barat reached 100, underscoring substantial regional imbalances in 
productive output.
Similarly, Java recorded the highest regional production capacity, whereas Kalimantan recorded the 
highest regional per-employee productivity.
The assessment on the source of growth, focusing primarily on the growth of TFP, identified Kalimantan 
Timur as the leading province, with an annual TFP growth rate approximately 2.8 times the overall 
provincial average, followed by Kepulauan Riau, Kalimantan Utara, DKI Jakarta, and Papua Barat.
Analyzing TFP growth across Indonesia’s seven regions reveals a striking divergence in economic 
performance. Papua and Kalimantan emerged as the nation’s leaders in TFP expansion, whereas Nusa 
Tenggara significantly lagged behind its counterparts.
Notably, despite boasting the highest production capacity (estimated at 0.09), Java’s TFP fell short of 
the regional average (0.1), underscoring a nuanced dynamic in Indonesia’s regional growth landscape.
These findings reaffirmed earlier assessments of growth drivers, emphasizing that physical inputs did 
not solely dictate TFP growth. Instead, it reflected the broader impact of intangible factors that might 
shape Indonesia’s productivity trajectory beyond conventional measures. Indeed, as shown in the case 
of the region of Nusa Tenggara, a high level of factor input, specifically capital input, did not directly 
translate into growth.
At the firm level, the analyses underscore the significant impact of firm size in shaping production and 
productivity growth. More importantly, small businesses have faced a recurring decline in GVA from 
2013 to 2022, contracting at an annual average rate of –9.88%. This downturn was primarily driven by 
negative TFP growth, estimated at –5.26%, which brought forth a decisive impact on the sector’s sharp 
output decline.
In contrast, large and medium-sized firms recorded an annual growth rate of 3.86% over a ten-year 
period (2013–22). However, negative TFP growth, estimated at –3.46%, played a substantial role in 
curbing overall output expansion, contributing to a decline of nearly 90%.
36	 Although Indonesia’s administrative provinces are 38, 4 provinces (particularly in the Province of Papua) were excluded from the 
analysis due to insufficient data; these provinces were created recently.


===== PAGE 59 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 43
PRODUCTIVITY AND GROWTH IN INDONESIA
Meanwhile, micro enterprises witnessed a periodic output growth of 4.24%, though this was similarly 
hindered by a negative TFP growth of –3.13%. These findings underscore the pivotal influence of TFP 
in shaping growth dynamics across businesses of varying sizes.
In sum, the analysis of TFP dynamics across large and medium-sized, small, and micro firms from 2013 
to 2022, based on the relative level model, has yielded several noteworthy findings. Large and medium-
sized firms demonstrated a relative TFP (rTFP) of 1.458, surpassing the benchmark average of 1 across 
all firm sizes. In contrast, small and micro firms recorded significantly lower rTFP values of 0.435 and 
0.403, respectively. This finding indicates that apart from large and medium-sized firms, TFP in the 
manufacturing sector negatively influenced growth, effectively counteracting the contributions of labor 
and capital inputs.
These insights underscore an urgent need for targeted policy interventions aimed at enhancing TFP 
among, especially, small and micro enterprises. Given that these firms constitute 99.9% of all businesses 
in Indonesia, productivity improvements in small and micro firms are essential to sustaining national 
economic growth.
This study also investigated how Indonesia’s productivity, specifically its competitiveness in the 
international setting, is affected.
A comparative analysis of growth components of some countries (i.e., China, Japan, Korea, Malaysia, 
Thailand, Vietnam, and Indonesia) provided valuable insights, particularly regarding the role of 
productivity in output growth. Among the nations examined, China, Japan, and Korea demonstrated 
rTFP levels exceeding the average of 1, reflecting stronger productivity performance. Conversely, 
Malaysia, Thailand, Vietnam, and Indonesia recorded relative TFP levels below 1, indicating relatively 
weaker productivity within these economies.
It is important to note that Indonesia’s rTFP was estimated at 0.818, surpassing Malaysia (0.569), 
Thailand (0.717), and Vietnam (0.226). This outcome suggests Indonesia’s comparatively stronger 
productivity position; however, with nearly half of Indonesia’s average growth over the past three 
decades attributable to TFP gains, the GOI must recognize and prioritize productivity improvements as 
a pillar of sustainable economic growth and global competitiveness.
A comparative analysis of Indonesia’s productivity relative to advanced economies yielded similar 
conclusions. Over the past three decades, the TFP growth across five major economies (Canada, 
Germany, France, the UK, and the U.S.) stood at approximately 24%, whereas that of Indonesia stood 
at a modest 10%. This sharp contrast underscores Indonesia’s relative inefficiency in driving economic 
expansion compared to its more advanced counterparts.
One particularly striking observation was the rTFP of the U.S., estimated at 2.08, far exceeding the 
other economies in comparison and underscoring its exceptional productivity levels. While the U.S. 
demonstrated superior efficiency with an rTFP above 1, the relative TFP of Canada, Germany, France, 
the UK, and Indonesia remained below this threshold. Indonesia’s rTFP, measured at 0.339, signaled a 
considerable gap in productivity growth.
Overall, these findings reinforced the urgent need for Indonesia to prioritize TFP growth as a core 
strategy for bolstering economic efficiency and sustaining long-term growth.

===== PAGE 61 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 45
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH  
AND PRODUCTIVITY
This master plan is divided into two main sections. The first section presents a comprehensive analysis of 
the economic growth, productivity, and industrial development nexus. Through the analysis, the master plan 
establishes a robust foundation to support the GOI in advancing evidence-based policymaking (EBPM).
The analysis thus far has identified key challenges that hinder the improvement of Indonesia’s 
productivity, which can be summarized as follows:
1.	 Dependence on labor and capital factor-driven growth, leading to inefficient resource use and allocation;
2.	 Uncompetitive human capital and inefficient investment;
3.	 Lagging technological capability;
4.	 Disparities across firm sizes, industrial sectors, and geographic regions; and
5.	 Complex regulatory framework and bureaucratic excess that hamper innovation.
The second section proposes a national vision, mission, and appended strategies for enhancing 
productivity that address the preceding challenges.
Vision
This master plan is built on the aspiration of enhancing productivity as the key driver to foster an 
innovative workforce and a competitive economy. It also aims to advance toward a forward-thinking 
nation of economic progress, aiming to achieve 8% growth in the medium term and maintaining an 
annual growth of at least 6% over the next decade. By doing so, Indonesia can gradually progress toward 
an advanced economy and ultimately attain the goals of the Visi Emas Indonesia 2045; hence, the vision 
(Figure 1): Smart People, Smart Economy, Smart Nation.
Mission
The following mission statement articulates guiding strategic directions for the advancement of the 
vision for national productivity enhancement:
Empower a highly skilled, innovation-driven workforce, strengthen a competitive economy through 
R&D excellence, and advance toward a forward-thinking nation at the forefront of technology.
Core Values
The core values shaping the vision, mission, and strategic pillars for national productivity enhancement 
are designed to embody the spirit of national development reflected in all development plans of 
Indonesia. These values include:
Innovation, productivity, sustainability, stability, global competitiveness, inclusiveness, and, above 
all, equal opportunities for all.


===== PAGE 62 =====

46 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Strategic Pillars
The strategic pillars outlined below will serve as key cornerstones, guiding and directing the achievement 
of the vision and mission set forth to enhance national productivity.
1.	 Mustering national consensus
2.	 Transitioning the national development paradigm to TFP-led growth
3.	 Strengthening infrastructure, digitalization, and intelligentization
4.	 Enhancing education and R&D
5.	 Diversifying and developing growth drivers.
The strategies devised above fundamentally act as mutually reinforcing and supportive initiatives 
(Figure 1).
Mustering National Consensus
As a paradigmatic shift in the nation’s development model is implemented, forging a national consensus 
must be treated as a top priority. Specifically, stakeholders and actors in the public and private sectors 
must be well informed, understand, and adopt the new growth paradigm to ensure cohesive efforts and 
collective action in moving toward unified goals.
Furthermore, coordination and cooperation among all ministries are essential for improving national 
productivity. This inter-ministerial task requires thorough understanding, acceptance, proactive 
participation, and practical implementation by all governmental agencies and institutions.
Transitioning Development Paradigm to TFP-led Growth
The preceding analyses indicated that Indonesia’s economic growth has closely mirrored changes in 
TFP. Indeed, growth and TFP demonstrated a strong correlation (r = 0.89). Historically, Indonesia’s 
growth has relied heavily on factor inputs; however, analyses indicate diminishing returns on these 
inputs, as observed in the case of the diminishing returns on investments in the Province of Bali. Thus,

===== PAGE 63 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 47
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
it is imperative to adopt a new growth paradigm (i.e., TFP-led growth), utilizing fewer resources while 
producing more.
Furthermore, TFP growth, particularly supported by technological advancements, leads to a more 
efficient use of labor and capital37; hence, TFP-led growth will lead to the redesign of the allocation 
and use of available resources. It will also contribute to reversing the decline in labor productivity and 
improving capital efficiency, thereby further enhancing growth through this mechanism.
Strengthening Infrastructure, Digital Transformation, and Intelligentization
Infrastructure catalyzes economic expansion. For example, a well-developed road network, robust 
communication systems, seamless energy supplies, and efficient water and sewer systems, among 
others, contribute to thriving economic activities.
Infrastructure also includes digital infrastructure, such as hardware, software, networks, data centers, 
cloud computing, cybersecurity systems, and similar components, constituting the backbone of today’s 
economy, particularly in the application of innovative digital frontier technologies such as artificial 
intelligence (AI), big data, blockchain, and other emerging tools across various socio-economic aspects. 
This process is referred to as intelligentization.
Infrastructure development will serve as a booster for national productivity by, inter alia, increasing 
efficiency, reducing production costs, ensuring timely delivery, facilitating trade, and enhancing 
workforce capacity.
Enhancing Education and R&D
Innovation is a primary driver of TFP and economic growth, being fundamentally supported by two 
core pillars: human capital and R&D. Education enhances human potential and fosters innovation. R&D 
advances knowledge while facilitating its transformation into products and services that cater to societal 
needs; therefore, sustained investment in education and R&D can foster continuous progress and long-
term economic development.
Investment in education transcends traditional school systems to encompass workforce training and 
ongoing professional development, thereby aligning with the rapidly changing demands of society and 
the economy. For example, continuing education (such as upskilling and reskilling initiatives) is crucial 
for sustaining workforce competency and enhancing adaptability to account for evolving labor market 
demands and technological changes.
Diversifying and Developing Growth Drivers
Indonesia’s economic growth has historically been driven by traditional industries, including agriculture, 
forestry and fishing, mining and quarrying, construction, wholesale and retail trade, and manufacturing. 
Manufacturing, in particular, has long supported the nation’s economic development; however, like 
other traditional industries, the sector shows signs of productivity decline. Therefore, the GOI must 
nurture emerging industries and leverage the potential of underdeveloped sectors to ensure sustained 
economic growth and long-term competitiveness.
Small and micro industries constitute the predominant business sector in Indonesia; however, enhancing 
their competitiveness remains a critical issue. Despite their predominance in the economic landscape, 
their contribution to overall output is significantly lower than that of large and medium-sized enterprises; 
therefore, targeted measures to strengthen their productivity and market presence are crucial for 
sustainable and equitable economic development.
Customized measures for developing local industries and economies are essential for enhancing regional 
economic capacity. These efforts will ultimately bridge regional gaps and foster equitable growth across 
different regions, contributing to the nation’s sustainable and balanced development.
37	 Deboeck, B. (2023). Prospects for long-term productivity growth. Quarterly Report on the Euro Area (QREA), 22(1), 31–42.


===== PAGE 64 =====

48 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Strategic Thrusts and Enablers/Initiators
This master plan has presented a vision, mission, and strategy for enhancing national productivity for 
the RPJMN 2025–2029 period, outlining the future direction of Bappenas, the national planning agency, 
to improve national productivity.
Bappenas has been responsible for planning and implementing national development strategies and is 
now committed to enhancing productivity and leading the nation toward a transition to a TFP-led growth 
phase. This shift in growth paradigm aims to achieve higher productivity and national competitiveness 
while establishing a foundation for sustainable and equitable economic development.
This section outlines specific priority areas that contribute to achieving the objectives of the strategic 
pillars, referred to as strategic thrusts. Additionally, it delineates illustrative initiatives and recommended 
actions for the line ministries and other public institutions to implement, serving as strategic enablers or 
initiators to drive progress toward these objectives.
A caveat must be noted: The strategic enablers and initiators outlined here serve primarily as illustrative 
examples. They intentionally exclude ongoing action plans and policy initiatives currently being 
implemented by line ministries and public institutions. Nonetheless, in select cases, references to 
action plans and policy initiatives are included where they complement and build upon existing efforts, 
particularly in areas where further alignment or emphasis may support broader national productivity.
Such an approach is adopted to minimize redundancy and avoid overlap with current action plans and 
initiatives. Accordingly, Bappenas will need to fine-tune and align these enablers and initiators with the 
most up-to-date policy landscape. In this context, a comprehensive vision matrix, including strategic 
thrusts and enablers/initiators, is provided in Appendix 1.
(Strategic Thrust) Enhancing Productivity Awareness by Advocating the National Movement 
of the 8-4-2-2 Movement
The Logic of the 8-4-2-2 Movement: As stated, it is a national imperative to strive for 8% growth 
during the RPJMN period (National Mid-term Development Plan, 2025–2029) while maintaining at 
least 6% growth over the following decade. Achieving these ambitious targets would require, ante 
omnia, fostering nationwide awareness, consensus, and understanding among public and private sector 
stakeholders and actors that productivity enhancement, and consequently economic growth, must be 
recognized as a national priority. Thus, this document recommends advocating, at the national level, for 
the “8-4-2-2 Movement,” derived from growth accounting principles.
This framework represents an 8% growth of national output, driven by contributions from labor input 
(4%), capital (2%), and TFP (2%). It can be expressed as: Output (8%) = Labor (4%) + Capital (2%) + 
TFP (2%).
Bappenas has set targeted annual growth rates for the period, as shown in Figure 2. Based on growth 
trajectories over the past decade, considering industrial sectors that influence TFP growth such as 
transportation, information and communication, education, and human health and social work activities, 
industry output is expected to reach the levels outlined in Table 2.
If output levels across the respective industrial sectors are maintained, the aggregate national annual 
output (Table 1) is projected to increase as follows: approximately 5.40% (2025) → 6.38% (2026) → 
7.50% (2027) → 7.78% (2028) → 8.17% (2029).
To achieve the targeted output, the respective industries will require factor inputs as presented in 
Table 2. Labor growth must be relatively high in sectors such as business activities (9.9%), accommodation 
and food service activities (9.7%), transportation and storage (8.8%), information and communication 
(8.5%), and human health and social work activities (8.4%).38
38	 The figures in parentheses represent the CAGR for the 2025–2029 period.

As shown in the preceding estimate, the increase in total TFP is driven by growth in key industrial sectors, 
including education, information and communication, public administration and defense, human health 
and social work activities, and manufacturing (Figure 3). Notably, strong TFP growth in the education and 
information and communication sectors serves as a catalyst for boosting TFP growth across other industries.
Another important point is the low TFP projection for the agriculture sector, which is estimated to stand at 
0.49% in 2029. Efforts to enhance sectoral productivity may fall short, as labor contribution in agriculture is 
projected to reach only 0.95% by 2029, despite the sector being the most labor-intensive within the national 
development framework. Nonetheless, given its role as the largest employer and its critical function in ensuring 
food security, the agriculture sector warrants greater attention from the GOI for productivity enhancement.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Disseminating the Significance of Productivity Enhancement for the 
Sustainable Development of the National Economy: Public participation significantly enhances 
the legitimacy and effectiveness of policymaking.40 Indeed, public acceptance, understanding, and 
engagement are crucial in ensuring the success of public policies; however, concerns persist that 
policymaking in Indonesia has not fully integrated public participation.41 Given the crucial role of

productivity enhancement in determining whether Indonesia can achieve developed-country status by

must be widely recognized, understood, and embraced by all facets of society.

KADIN, Kamar Dagang dan Industri Indonesia (Chamber of Commerce and Industry, Indonesia)
In collaboration with the Ministry of Home Affairs, Bappenas (the lead agency overseeing the 
dissemination of the 8-4-2-2 Movement) must actively promote public participation. They should utilize 
a diverse range of communication channels to engage the public, including social media platforms (SNS), 
television, radio, street banners and displays as well as seminars, conferences, and similar kinds of outreach 
initiatives. These tools could ensure widespread awareness and foster meaningful participation in the 
dissemination process, through which Bappenas and the Ministry of Home Affairs can cultivate greater 
public acceptance by ensuring widespread awareness and engagement with the movement. Moreover, 
they can integrate societal perspectives and foster open dialogue to align the movement with the diverse 
needs and expectations of different sectors of society and reinforce its relevance and acceptance. This 
approach aims to promote the active and voluntary participation of all socio-economic actors in enhancing 
productivity and strengthening collective efforts toward sustainable national development.
(Strategic Thrust) Reducing Productivity Loss
Productivity loss generally refers to decreased efficiency or output, often manifesting in the form of 
missed delivery deadlines, delays, reduced production, and compromised quality of goods and services 
due to rushed work.42 Productivity loss can stem from multiple factors, such as inefficient workflows, 
the absence of appropriate tools, poor time management, and similar inefficiencies.43 Therefore, to 
ensure sustained efficiency and competitiveness, minimizing or preventing productivity loss is just as 
crucial as enhancing productivity.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Fostering a Corps of Productivity Coaches and Consultants, Utilizing 
Retirees with Experience in the Relevant Fields of Industry: Productivity and competitiveness hinge 
on deep knowledge capital, and Indonesia possesses an underutilized asset in its retired industrial 
professionals. These individuals carry decades of accumulated technical wisdom, managerial insight, 
and sectoral expertise, assets too valuable to remain dormant. By systematically mobilizing this pool 
of experience and expertise, the government can foster a corps of productivity coaches and consultants 
to serve as catalysts for industrial upgrading, operational efficiency, and technology absorption across 
priority sectors. This approach can bridge Indonesia’s capability gap with minimal lead-time, high 
contextual relevance, and strong mentorship continuity.
An Illustration: Challenges and Policy Gaps in Indonesia’s Textile Industry
The challenges faced by Indonesia’s textile industry can serve as an instructive example of why the 
country’s industrial sector is losing its competitive edge; one of the most significant contributing factors 
is the lack of innovation, coupled with declining output and product quality, primarily due to the use of 
obsolete machinery.
42	 Shrivastava, P. (2025). What Is Lost Productivity? Tips To Handle Productivity Loss. May 12, 2025, EmpMonitor. Retrieved 26 May 2025, 
from https://empmonitor.com/blog/what-is-lost-productivity/
43	 Ibid.

===== PAGE 72 =====

56 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Reports indicate that approximately 80% of Indonesia’s textile production machines are over 25 years 
old, which severely impacts efficiency and global competitiveness.44 Furthermore, 5 million of the 
approximately 7.8 million spinning machines have been in operation for over two decades. Moreover, 
an estimated 82% of weaving machines and 93% of finishing industry machines are considered 
obsolete, hindering productivity and modernization efforts.45 Thus, many businesses have either scaled 
back operations or relocated to other emerging countries within the region, particularly Vietnam and 
Bangladesh, both of which have risen as global market leaders (alongside China) through technological 
advancements and modernization efforts.46
Furthermore, incentives for entrepreneurs in the textile sector to replace and upgrade aging machinery 
are insufficient. Indonesia has moved beyond a low-wage, labor-intensive production model, rendering 
it increasingly unviable to sustain operations without adopting and embracing more advanced 
manufacturing technologies.47 Without targeted support and a strategic shift toward modernization, the 
industry may risk falling further behind its more adaptive regional competitors.
The GOI provides financial support to the textile industry, such as electricity subsidies, to alleviate 
operational burdens; however, certain critical aspects such as fostering innovation and optimizing 
workflow management cannot be directly subsidized.
Re-activating Knowledge Capital
To address the knowledge gap discussed above, the GOI should establish a specialized corps of 
“innovation consultants,” comprising experienced retired professionals with extensive expertise in 
the textile sector. These consultants would mentor, coach, and advise industry stakeholders, guiding 
businesses in adopting modernized production techniques, improving efficiency, and implementing 
innovative strategies to enhance productivity and competitiveness.
Countries such as Korea and Japan have successfully adopted this approach, where dedicated private-
sector institutions provide industry-specific consulting services. Moreover, organizations such as the 
Korea Management and Technology Consultants Association and the Japan Management Consultants 
Association play a crucial role in bridging industry experts with businesses seeking professional 
consulting, advisory, and coaching services. Their expertise spans a wide range of areas, including 
management strategy, innovation, financial facilitation, accounting and finance, market development, 
production optimization, cost reduction, IT enhancement, and other critical business areas.48
These experts provide tailored guidance and strategic insights, primarily derived from work site 
observations and analyses, significantly contributing to fostering competitiveness. Implementing a 
similar framework in Indonesia could help the textile sector, or other manufacturing industrial sectors, 
benefit from structured expertise, mitigating the challenges posed by aging machinery and outdated 
production methods.
This model adopted the textile industry as an illustrative case; however, it can be effectively extended 
to the agricultural sector, which faces similar, if not identical, challenges. Much like the textile industry, 
Indonesia’s agricultural sector struggles with outdated machinery, inefficient workflows, and slow 
modernization, all of which hinder productivity and competitiveness. Many farmers, particularly 
smallholder groups, lack the financial resources to upgrade essential equipment such as husking and 
milling machines, leading to production backlogs and reduced product quality.

Training and certification are crucial components of this initiative, overseen by the Ministry of Manpower

ensure that consultants have the required expertise to support businesses effectively.
International development partners, including the UNDP, ILO, GIZ, JICA, and KOICA, can play a crucial

They can also support the design of training-of-trainers (ToT) modules for productivity coaches, share

building programs and digital platforms for consultant deployment.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Enhancing the Food Waste Conversion Program into Organic 
Fertilizer and Animal Feed toward a National-Level Recycling System as Part of an Active 
Promotion of a Circular and Green Economy: As Indonesia deepens its commitment to a circular and 
green economy, scaling up food waste conversion into organic fertilizer and animal feed offers a high-
impact, low-tech solution to reduce environmental burden and enhance resource efficiency. Transforming 
this initiative into a national-level system can divert tonnes of biodegradable waste from landfills, 
bolster local farming productivity, reduce reliance on chemical inputs, and stimulate green 
entrepreneurship at the community level.
Scaling Food Waste Recovery for Sustainable Resource Use
Food waste is one of Indonesia’s most pressing socio-economic challenges, with significant economic 
and environmental consequences. Some statistics suggest that the economic loss resulting from food 
waste ranges between IDR213–551 trillion annually, equivalent to about 4%–5% of Indonesia’s GDP 
per year, underscoring the severity of the issue.49 Some even argue that its impact is as concerning as 
that of plastic waste.50
Indonesia’s per capita food waste (20–77 kg) is lower than that of Malaysia (40–102 kg) and Thailand 
(69–106 kg); however, the sheer size of its population results in a total nationwide waste volume that surpasses 
that of other ASEAN countries.51 Consequently, Indonesia stands as the most significant contributor to food 
waste in the region, highlighting the urgent need to strengthen food waste management solutions.
One of the most viable solutions to addressing food waste today includes minimizing waste at its source 
(such as during food preparation and processing) and re-purposing inedible food waste into valuable 
resources such as animal feed and fertilizers (Figure 4). This approach reduces environmental impact and 
enhances resource efficiency by transforming discarded food into productive inputs for other industries.
The socio-economic benefits of food waste recovery are substantial. Strengthening the waste management 
sector can create jobs, bolster local economies, and alleviate the financial/resource burdens associated 
with producing animal feed and fertilizers.52 Furthermore, this initiative directly supports the systemic 
implementation of Green Economy principles by promoting sustainable resource recycling and circular 
economy practices.
Establishing infrastructure for food waste separation, collection, transportation, and processing is 
essential for an effective food waste recovery system (Figure 4). It is also important to ensure that 
animal and crop health are safeguarded throughout the process, necessitating strict quality control and 
regulatory measures. As such, successfully implementing this system requires the active participation of 
multiple stakeholders, including government agencies, private enterprises, farmers’ community groups, 
NGOs, and research institutions.

(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Establishing and expanding small- and medium-scale rice processing 
centers (RPCs) in each district to reduce postharvest loss: Establishing and scaling small- and medium-
scale RPCs at the district level is a strategic intervention to reduce postharvest losses and improve rice 
quality. These facilities can enhance efficiency in drying, milling, and packaging processes, enabling 
farmers to recover value lost during postharvest stages while simultaneously strengthening food security 
and promoting rural agro-industrial development.
Rethinking Rice Self-Sufficiency through Investment in Process
As it successfully did in 2017 and again between 2019 and 2021, the GOI has set an ambitious goal 
of producing 32 million tonnes of rice by June 2025 to achieve self-sufficiency.54 This commitment 
has become increasingly crucial given the recent food crises in Malaysia, Japan, and other regions, 
reinforcing the urgency of ensuring food security and price stability. Indonesia’s rice imports surged to 
approximately 4.5 million tonnes in 2024, making the challenge more urgent than ever.
While increasing the production capacity of rice remains a priority, reducing harvest and postharvest 
losses is equally vital. Indonesia currently experiences an estimated 20.51% loss across various stages 
of rice production, including harvesting (9.52%), threshing (4.78%), drying (2.13%), and milling 
(2.19%);55 however, in reality, the total loss may exceed 30%.56 As a matter of course, these losses 
diminish farmers’ incomes, exacerbate food insecurity, and contribute to broader economic losses at the 
national level.57
These losses primarily stem from the continued use of outdated, non-standardized machinery and rice 
milling units that fail to implement proper sanitation and hygiene protocols, compounded by reliance 
on traditional agricultural practices. Such obsolete milling equipment and the lack of standard operation 
procedures clearly contribute to weight loss and a decline in rice quality.
Indonesia faces significant challenges in formulating comprehensive policy frameworks to address 
the root causes of food loss and waste while simultaneously promoting a more efficient and resilient 
agricultural system. In this context, the development of Rice Processing Centers (RPCs) represents a 
strategic policy response to the persistent issues surrounding postharvest rice processing.
An RPC is a facility that manages the entire rice production process, including drying, storage, milling, 
inspection, and sales, within a single location. Unlike traditional methods conducted at the individual 
farm level, RPCs employ large-scale automated systems to enhance efficiency in postharvest operations.
RPCs reduce labor requirements, enhance rice quality, and improve distribution efficiency, assuming a 
pivotal role in the rice supply chain that contributes to increased farm incomes and facilitates the direct 
provision of high-quality rice to consumers. 
In this context, investing in measures to reduce post-harvest losses—such as enhancing the performance 
of RPCs, improving rice quality, boosting yields, and developing market segments—offers a more 
rational approach to sustaining productivity than simply allocating additional resources to expand rice 
production.

BRIN, Badan Riset dan Inovasi Nasional (National Research and Innovation Agency)
(Strategic Thrust) Improving Regulatory Stability
Indonesia’s complex and fragmented regulatory environment is a critical barrier to innovation, 
investment, and broader economic development. Persistent regulatory uncertainty, coupled with 
overlapping and often contradictory rules and regulations, creates significant obstacles for innovative 
business activities at the national level and across sub-national jurisdictions.58 As such, Indonesia’s 
overall regulatory landscape faces a critical governance challenge.
Furthermore, institutional fragmentation and compartmentalization exacerbate the complexity of 
Indonesia’s regulatory environment. Numerous regulatory bodies operate with insufficient coordination, 
and information sharing between national and sub-national authorities remains limited. This siloed 
structure stems from a lack of coordination, resulting in a breakdown in information flow between 
national and local levels, severely limiting national oversight of the entire regulatory regime (Figure 5).
Such fragmentation and inadequate coordination undermine policy coherence, resulting in inconsistent 
regulations, overlapping mandates, and administrative inefficiencies. Unsurprisingly, these conflicts 
hinder effective policy implementation, risk eroding public trust and confidence, and discourage private 
sector participation and engagement.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Establishing a Dedicated Institution to Address Regulatory Stability: 
Reducing regulatory uncertainty and improving intergovernmental coordination requires a dedicated 
public agency with the following core mandates:
1.	 Systematically catalog, monitor, and assess existing regulations at national and sub-national levels.
2.	 Initiate amendment and deregulation efforts aimed at achieving regulatory stability by resolving the 
fragmentation, overlaps, and contradictions within the nation’s body of regulations that currently 
undermine policy coherence.
The agency’s mandate also includes outdated regulations to accommodate and promote innovative business 
models as well as the adoption of technology across industries. This approach can be accomplished through 
58	 World Bank. (2024b). Unleashing Indonesia’s business potential. June 2024, Washington, D.C., p.34.

NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
a combination of (1) amending existing frameworks, (2) introducing new regulatory provisions, and 
(3) implementing regulatory sandboxes to test and refine emerging practices within a controlled setting.
Commissioned with these mandates, the proposed Innovative Regulation Commission of Indonesia (tentative 
designation) should be established as a presidential commission reporting directly to the president, given 
that its cross-sectoral authority spans ministries and state institutions. Its establishment could be formalized 
through a presidential regulation (peraturan presiden), granting legal authority to oversee inter-ministerial 
regulatory reform and streamline regulatory inventories across all levels of government.

ministries (outlined in Table 3-5) and independent regulatory experts appointed by the chairperson to 
ensure legitimacy and technical rigor. The minister of National Development Planning/Bappenas should 
serve as the ex officio chairperson of the Commission.

The House of Representatives (Dewan Perwakilan Rakyat [DPR]) and the Ministry of Law (MOL) play 
pivotal roles in this initiative, and their support is essential. The DPR serves as a legislative body and an 
oversight authority in creating and operating the proposed government agency. It reviews and enacts the 
laws that formally establish the Commission, approves its budget, and ensures financial transparency. 
Moreover, the DPR safeguards the public interest by ensuring that the Commission addresses diverse 
regional and societal needs.
In contrast, the MOL is responsible for initiating and shaping the legal foundation of the proposed 
Commission. It drafts the enabling legislation, outlines the structural and operational framework, and 
ensures alignment with national legal standards and regulations. The MOL also provides ongoing 
legal support, particularly in the Commission’s work to catalog existing regulations, amend outdated 
provisions, and develop new regulatory frameworks (such as the implementation of a regulatory 
sandbox) to foster innovation and responsiveness.
(Strategic Thrust) Strengthening Forward-Looking Infrastructure
Forward-looking is commonly understood as “planning for the future, especially in a new or creative 
way.”59 In this context, forward-looking infrastructure refers to foundational systems, physical and 
institutional, designed to support sustainable socio-economic activity and enhance long-term productivity.
Many countries have adopted the forward-looking infrastructure concept to define their national 
development strategies, aiming to enhance future competitiveness. These efforts address pressing 
challenges to development, including climate change, regional disparities, and the need for enhanced 
digital connectivity.
Similarly, Indonesia has pursued infrastructure policies focused on future preparedness, emphasizing 
sustainability, disaster resilience, and digital transformation. Key initiatives include enhancing early 
warning systems, implementing green infrastructure to bolster urban resilience, and formulating 
comprehensive national disaster management frameworks. Furthermore, Indonesia is increasingly 
prioritizing the expansion of digital infrastructure as a driver of economic growth and structural 
modernization; thus, continued commitment to such initiatives is vital for strengthening forward-
looking infrastructure and boosting national productivity, particularly TFP.
(Strategic Enabler/initiator) Enhancing Smart Disaster Preparedness and Early Warning Systems: 
Indonesia is prone to natural disasters due to its geomorphological characteristics as an archipelago. 
Located at the convergence of the Pacific, Eurasian, and Indo-Australian tectonic plates, it experiences 
frequent volcanic activity, earthquakes, and tsunamis. The country is estimated to have around 130 
active volcanoes, the highest number for any nation globally.60 Moreover, Indonesia’s geographical and 
geological features, combined with its rugged topography, increase the chance of floods and landslides, 
particularly during the annual monsoon season.61
Building a Resilient Indonesia through Early Warning Infrastructure
Natural disasters in Indonesia have resulted in substantial human and economic consequences, with 
significant loss of life, widespread injuries, and large-scale displacement. Between 2001 and 2010, the 
annual economic damage was estimated at approximately USD1.5 billion, equivalent to around 0.3% 
of the national GDP.62 These recurring disasters also place a significant burden on the state budget, as 
substantial public funds must be diverted toward emergency response and postdisaster recovery.
Thus, implementing early warning systems (EWSs) is critical, as they can significantly reduce loss of 
life and economic damage. For example, during the 2010 eruption of Mt. Merapi on Java Island, timely 
59	 Cambridge Dictionary. (2025). Cambridge dictionary academic content dictionary. Cambridge University Press & Assessment.

25 April 2025 from timesofindia.com. https://timesofindia.indiatimes.com/world/

alerts issued by the government facilitated the evacuation of approximately 70,000 people, resulting in 
around 353 fatalities.63, 64
EWS permits multiple, interrelated benefits that collectively mitigate productivity decline. By 
significantly reducing mortality and injury, limiting economic losses, safeguarding critical infrastructure, 
and strengthening overall disaster resilience, it helps maintain productivity amid adverse events.65
The previous chapter discussed how Indonesia’s economic growth in 1982 (when the country faced a 
disaster triggered by the eruption of Mount Galunggung in West Java) was significantly hindered by 
TFP, which reduced growth by approximately 50.2%. In contrast, countries that did not experience such 
disasters experienced far less severe productivity setbacks during the same year. For example, India 
experienced a modest strain on growth, with a negative TFP contribution of approximately 9%. At the 
same time, Japan benefited from a substantial productivity boost, with TFP contributing positively to 
growth by around 29% (Table 4).
Assuming that proactive natural disaster prevention programs and subsequent disaster risk reduction 
measures had been fully implemented in Indonesia at the time (thereby reducing the adverse impact of 
TFP by up to 50%), the country’s economic growth rate in 1982 could have risen to approximately 3.6%. 
Although hypothetical, this scenario underscores the crucial importance of institutional preparedness and 
resilient infrastructure in maintaining productivity and promoting macroeconomic stability, particularly 
in a disaster-prone country.

Investing in EWS for Future Resilience
Despite ongoing efforts by the GOI to establish and enhance EWS, the development and implementation 
of these systems remains a challenge. Budgets for EWS have been declining, and the process of acquiring 
necessary resources remains a bureaucratically complex one; thus, a primary concern is securing 
adequate funding.66 Moreover, enhancing public awareness and strengthening institutional capacity are 
essential for mitigating the impact of disasters, particularly in minimizing adverse effects on TFP.

With the rapid evolution of digital technologies (AI, IoT, and real-time data analytics, among others),

framework. To maximize their effectiveness, this integration should be accompanied by a comprehensive 
reform of the existing governance structure, particularly streamlining communication pathways and 
addressing fragmented decision-making processes. These reforms are crucial for enhancing operational 
coordination and responsiveness during times of crisis. Above all, the GOI must recognize that spending 
on disaster preparedness is not a mere expenditure but a strategic investment in promoting national 
resilience and long-term competitiveness.

(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Grading Up Existing Ports to Smart and Intelligent Ports: As an archipelagic 
state, Indonesia’s geographical configuration necessitates a reliance on maritime transportation as a 
fundamental mechanism for ensuring territorial integration and facilitating participation in the global 
supply chain. Within this framework, ports constitute critical national infrastructure as gateways for 
international and domestic trade and facilitators for employment generation and the development of 
value-added economic activities. Empirical evidence underscores this significance: in 2017, Indonesia’s 
four state-owned ports, operated by PT Pelabuhan Indonesia (Pelindo) I, II, III, and IV, contributed 
approximately USD130 million to the national economy, accounting for 0.21% of the country’s GDP.67 
As such, the competitiveness of ports bears considerable socio-economic and strategic importance for 
Indonesia’s long-term development agenda.
Reclaiming Indonesia’s Logistics Edge in the Digital Port Era
Recognizing the strategic importance of port competitiveness, the GOI has implemented a series of 
initiatives to enhance the operational efficiency of its port infrastructure. These initiatives include the 
deployment of smart technologies at key facilities such as Kuala Tanjung, Cikarang Dry Port, Tanjung

The development of a port’s competitive advantage is largely determined by the availability and 
accessibility of physical and technical infrastructure, the efficiency of operational processes, and 
the extent of connectivity with hinterland networks.71 Accordingly, enhancing port competitiveness 
constitutes a complex agenda that demands a sustained, long-term policy commitment from multiple 
actors and stakeholders. Nonetheless, transitioning to smart systems represents a feasible objective 
within the near-to-medium term72; therefore, the GOI’s initiative to prioritize smart port development 
may be viewed as a prudent and forward-looking strategic direction.
However, by 2023, only 14 out of the 149 targeted ports (approximately 9%) had fully transitioned to 
smart and green port operations.73 This sluggish progress is largely attributable to an investment shortfall 
in technological infrastructure and a significant gap in the availability of qualified human resources, 
particularly in areas such as information technology and digital port management.74, 75 Furthermore, the 
existing workforce demonstrates limited capacity to comprehend and operate within a digitalized port 
system, highlighting the urgent need for targeted upskilling initiatives.76

(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Developing Techno-Parks across Provinces to Bridge Regional Disparities

enterprise competitiveness, and reinforcing the capacity of knowledge-intensive institutions79; however,

not met the expected benchmarks associated with successful STPs in developed countries that have

Despite its initial promise, the STP initiative in Indonesia failed to achieve the anticipated momentum. As newer 
and more urgent development priorities emerged, the program receded from the policy spotlight, becoming 
a case of “quo vadis.”81 A critical factor was its detachment from local communities, which undermined its 
embeddedness and compromised its long-term sustainability within Indonesia’s socio-economic context.82
Rethinking STP in the Indonesian Developmental Landscape
Although Indonesia has encountered various setbacks, the strategic potential of STPs in leading regional 
economic development remains intact. In many countries, STPs serve as catalysts for reducing regional 
disparities by fostering collaboration among local industries, universities, and research institutions. 
They also facilitate the creation of start-ups, business incubation, enterprise development, and other 
innovation-led activities.
Therefore, it is necessary to revive the STP scheme; however, this time, it must be redesigned on a smaller 
scale and guided by redefined objectives, specifically through the “techno-park (TP)” model at the city 
or regency level, as outlined in the RPJMN.83 The TP model better aligns with the Indonesian context by 
establishing stronger linkages with surrounding communities and community-based industries.
These TPs can serve as strategic platforms to bridge the urban–rural divide in e-literacy, particularly 
regarding access to and fluency in advanced digital technologies, including blockchain, AI, IoT, and 
related competencies. In this sense, TPs can serve as incubators for narrowing urban–rural economic 
disparities arising from what some researchers termed as the “next-level digital divide.”84

Digital penetration, as proxied by Internet usage in rural areas over the last decade, has increased 
significantly, with the rural–urban gap narrowing from 29.7% in 2014 to just 3.9% in 2023 (Figure 6). 
However, rural Indonesia continues to face a shortage of robust digital infrastructure, which hampers 
equitable access, meaningful digital participation, and the integration of digital skills into daily life and 
business practices.85
Accordingly, the scale, objectives, and operational structure of TPs must be recalibrated across 
provinces. The benefits are evident: TPs can serve as platforms for narrowing urban–rural digital 
literacy gaps while also acting as engines for regional economic development by supporting local start-
ups, business incubators, and business development. Moreover, they can facilitate knowledge transfer 
between research institutions and small businesses, generate higher-quality employment opportunities, 
stimulate innovation ecosystems tailored to local needs and strengths, and enhance digital inclusion 
through community-based technology design.

stakeholders, whose coordinated roles are essential to the effective planning, development, and 
sustainability of TPs across Indonesia.

(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Empowering MSMEs to Lead Indonesia’s Smart Factory Transformation:. 
As is widely recognized, micro and small businesses dominate Indonesia’s business landscape, accounting 
for around 98.5% of all enterprises. In contrast, only 1.35% are medium-sized businesses, and a mere 
0.1% are classified as large-scale enterprises. This pattern is similarly reflected in the manufacturing 
85	 Akbar, M., & Wijaya, G. (2024, June). Digital literacy of rural areas in Indonesia: Challenges and opportunities. In Proceedings of the 4th

sector: out of approximately 4.3 million firms, microenterprises constitute 93.3%, small-sized firms 
6.0%, and medium and large enterprises together represent only 0.7%.86 Concerning employment, 
micro and small enterprises account for approximately 60.8% of the workforce, with medium and large 
enterprises employing the remaining 39.2% (See Section 3-5 in Chapter 3).
However, the picture shifts significantly when examining GVA. Medium and large enterprises contribute 
around 93.1%, equivalent to approximately IDR3 quadrillion, whereas small enterprises contribute 
1.8% (IDR58 trillion) and micro enterprises 5.1% (IDR163 trillion).87 These values indicate that 
medium and large enterprises generate roughly 13.6 times more GVA compared with micro and small 
enterprises combined.
Bridging the MSME Productivity Gap through Smart Manufacturing
Analyzing the preceding data reveals that the per-worker productivity of micro and small enterprises 
is significantly lower, estimated at nearly one-tenth, than that of medium and large enterprises. This 
productivity gap poses a serious challenge to Indonesia’s national economy, as micro and small 
enterprises constitute the dominant business segment and are widely regarded as the backbone of the 
Indonesian economy. The lower workforce productivity in Indonesia’s manufacturing MSMEs is also 
evident in international comparisons, where it trails behind regional competitors.88
This persistent productivity gap highlights the urgent need for targeted reforms to enhance the 
performance of Indonesia’s MSMEs. Limited access to financial resources has persisted as a key 
constraint for Indonesian MSMEs; however, their adoption of technology has remained a central policy 
priority within the Making Indonesia 4.0 roadmap.
In the past, government policies on technology adoption have primarily focused on LEs; however, a 
strategic shift of the policy focus is imperative, as MSMEs are fundamental actors in the industrial 
ecosystem. National competitiveness in the current manufacturing landscape cannot be fully realized if 
MSMEs are left behind in the transition to modernized production systems.
Recognizing the strategic imperative of MSME digital transformation to promote inclusive growth 
through digitalization, the GOI has enacted several policy packages aimed at accelerating SMEs’ digital 
adoption89; however, structural impediments persist. These issues include constrained financial access 
(exacerbated by high initial capital requirements and the scarcity of tailored financial instruments), 
insufficient digital literacy among the workforce, underdeveloped digital infrastructure, and resistance 
to organizational changes.90
Empirical evidence from smart factories in Indonesia remains limited; however, international experience, 
such as that of Korea, indicates that initial setup costs may be offset over the medium term, with cost 
recovery often observed within several years, depending on firm size and digital readiness. Notably, 
Korea’s deployment of over 30,000 smart factories has resulted in significant improvements in firm 
performance, including an average 25% increase in overall productivity. This gain reflects a 27% 
reduction in defect rates, a 29% decrease in operating costs, and a 19% reduction in delivery delays. 
Furthermore, critical, yet non-measurable, gains occurred in customer satisfaction.91 Moreover, the 
deployment of this innovative manufacturing system contributes to the overall increase in TFP as well.92

In light of the above, the GOI should recalibrate its smart factory policy to better align with emerging 
technological imperatives. Given the imperative to enhance domestic and international competitiveness 
in manufacturing productivity, Indonesia must pursue and strengthen its policy of smartization and 
intelligentization. As part of this effort, the GOI could prioritize the transformation of approximately 10,000 
high-potential manufacturing MSMEs to achieve at least Level 2 smart factory status during the RPJMN 
2025–2029 period, thereby re-igniting the foundational momentum for a digitally empowered 
manufacturing sector.

actors/stakeholders.

Bappenas will lead the promotion of this initiative, collaborating with the Ministry of Industry and the

The participation of private-sector firms is a critical determinant in ensuring the successful 
institutionalization of the initiative. Furthermore, the engagement of international development partners, 
many of whom have contributed to the establishment of smart factories in Indonesia through pilot 
programs and technical assistance, will enrich and strengthen the promotion of this initiative.
(Strategic Thrust) Augmenting Linkage between R&D and Commercialization
Fundamentally, technology is created to be used by human beings, the quintessential users of tools. Humans 
are inherently technological beings: creatures who use tools to “complement, enhance, or disburden 
[themselves].”93 While technology takes many forms, its actual value surfaces only when it is actively 
applied to solve problems and improve the human condition. In today’s world, such value, rooted in 
problem-solving and human betterment, is increasingly recognized and rewarded through market-based 
mechanisms, commonly referred to as technology commercialization. In this context, if a technology 
is not commercialized, it may lose its practical value at its core; hence, the importance of technology 
commercialization lies in its ability to transform inventions and innovations into practical applications.
93	 University of Twente. (2017). Human-Technology Relations. Department of Philosophy. Retrieved 02 May 2025, from  
https://www.utwente.nl/en/bms/phil/research/


===== PAGE 88 =====

72 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
In the context of national development, particularly in developing countries, technology 
gains value through a dynamic cycle. This cycle begins with government-funded R&D, 
which generates inventions and innovations that are then transferred to firms through 
structured 
mechanisms, 
enabling 
their 
commercialization 
into 
products 
and 
services. As 
these inventions and innovations enter the market, they generate economic returns, including 
tax revenues, which can be reinvested in further R&D (Figure  7). This situation creates a virtuous 
cycle of technology transfer and commercialization, reinforcing the capacity for invention/innovation 
and national development.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Reorganizing the National R&D Structure to Distinguish Between 
Short- and Long-Term Priorities: Indonesia’s level of technology commercialization appears 
relatively low compared with its regional peers, as suggested by indicators such as the share of 
medium- and high-tech manufactured exports and the total value of high-tech exports (Figure 8). 
In 2022, medium- and high-tech products accounted for approximately 32% of Indonesia’s 
manufactured exports. This figure lags behind that of Malaysia (63.2%), the Philippines 
(80.0%), Thailand (61.9%), and Vietnam (57.5%) (Figure 8[a]).
In terms of export value, Indonesia’s high-tech exports totaled around USD10.3 billion, 
significantly lower than those of Malaysia (USD136.8 billion) and Vietnam (USD135.9 billion). 
The Philippines and Thailand also outperformed Indonesia, with high-tech export values of 
USD41.7 billion and USD53.8 billion, respectively (Figure 8[b]).
While these indicators should be interpreted with caution as they do not capture domestically 
transacted forms of technology commercialization, such as e-commerce, FinTech, or other 
internal digital transformations, they nonetheless offer a valuable glimpse into the broader scale 
and intensity of a country’s technological commercialization.

NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Recalibrating R&D Ecosystem for Technology Commercialization
Numerous scholars, business leaders, and policy-makers, within and outside Indonesia, have attributed the 
country’s limited technology commercialization to a range of structural and regulatory constraints. These 
include institutional fragmentation, chronically low investment in R&D, a predominance of supply-driven 
research agendas, underdeveloped intellectual property (IP) commercialization ecosystems, and a persistent 
disconnect between industry and R&D institutions, among other systemic barriers.94, 95
The GOI responded to these R&D challenges by restructuring the national research landscape. In 
2021, it consolidated its research institutions; some have likened this approach (albeit only partially 
accurately) to Korea’s R&D institutional model, particularly the National Research Council of Science 
and Technology (NST).96 In contrast, Korea’s NST is primarily mandated by law to support, promote, 
systematically manage, and evaluate a network of government-funded science and technology research 
institutes rather than to centralize them under a single operational body.97
The centralized body, BRIN, has expanded significantly in scope and complexity; however, it remains 
an open question whether such a centralized R&D structure can respond with the agility required in a 
world shaped by rapid technological change and mounting global and domestic uncertainties.98 In other 
words, the defining feature of contemporary R&D, institutional agility, may be challenging to ensure.
Furthermore, the change in R&D institutional structure appears to be only a partial response to the 
country’s broader innovation and coordination challenges; the critical linkage between industry and 
R&D institutions remains weak, limiting the potential for research to generate tangible economic value.
Responsibility for R&D activities should be shared between BRIN and autonomous, independent 
research institutes closely linked to line ministries, based on the research timeframe and strategic scope 
(Table 15). This structure can address concerns around institutional agility and strengthen the linkage 
between industry and R&D, thereby enhancing the commercialization of research and technology.

NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
These autonomous and independent research institutes are well-positioned to manage short-term research 
(typically under 12 months) that directly supports their regulatory, operational, and sectoral expertise and 
mandates. Given the proximity of line ministries to industry and frontline policy implementation, autonomous 
and independent research institutes are uniquely positioned to undertake time-sensitive projects that require 
rapid results to inform decision-making and respond to urgent national priorities or evolving industry needs.
In contrast, BRIN, as the flagship of Indonesia’s research institutions, is better suited to undertake 
long-term, cross-sectoral, and frontier research. These projects often involve increased uncertainty, 
require extensive coordination across disciplines, and align with national development goals that 
exceed the annual policy cycle. Such initiatives include multi-year research on quantum communication 
technologies, aerospace technologies, national-scale modeling for future energy transitions, and other 
emerging or transformative technologies. BRIN’s consolidated expertise, infrastructure, and mandate 
support the development of high-impact innovations and long-horizon scientific advancement.

To prevent institutional fragmentation and compartmentalization, the following coordination and 
management mechanisms are recommended to bridge potential gaps and ensure seamless collaboration.
	•
Joint programming committees can align research priorities and coordinate funding streams across 
institutions.
	•
Project classification frameworks can categorize research initiatives based on duration, risk level, and 
policy relevance.
	•
Referral protocols can facilitate project reallocation when the scope or timeframes evolve beyond the 
original parameters.
This division of labor, mirrored in institutional arrangements found in Japan and the EU, recognizes the 
comparative strengths of each model: strategic foresight and central coordination on the one hand, and 
sectoral agility and operational responsiveness on the other (operational immediacy and sectoral insight 
within line ministries, and strategic foresight and long-horizon planning within BRIN) while enhancing 
the agility, coherence, and responsiveness of Indonesia’s R&D ecosystem.
Key Actors and Stakeholders
To ensure the successful implementation of this initiative, the active participation of key stakeholders is 
essential during the design phase and throughout the implementation, monitoring, adaptation, evaluation, 
and sustainability assessment phases.
In partnership with BRIN and the Cabinet Secretariat, Bappenas will take the lead, with support from 
other key stakeholders. Inputs from the public and, particularly, the private sector are crucial. Therefore, 
ensuring the active participation of universities, LEs, MSMEs, and KADIN (the Chamber of Commerce 
and Industry) is of particular importance.

BRIN, Badan Riset dan Inovasi Nasional (National Research and Innovation Agency)
(Strategic Thrust) Future-Ready Education in an Era of Technological Frontiers
Education is a fundamental driver of economic development, shaping the quality of human capital; 
its role has become even more critical in today’s rapidly changing world. The workforce must 
continually adapt to the global environment, particularly in response to the accelerating pace of 
technological change. Innovations in mobile computing, AI, cloud infrastructure, IoT, blockchain, and 
even quantum computing are swiftly being adopted across global manufacturing and service sectors; 
hence, this technological evolution presents immense opportunities as well as significant threats. 
Economies that cannot remain adaptive and responsive risk being left behind in an increasingly 
competitive global arena.
The rapid pace of digital transformation has redefined the demands of the modern workforce, with 
employers seeking individuals who are digitally literate and capable of continuously adapting to new 
tools, platforms, and innovations.
Nonetheless, education systems often struggle to keep pace with this technological evolution, and 
a growing disconnect has emerged between the skills imparted through formal education and those 
required by the labor market. Unless this gap is addressed, a mismatch between graduates and job 
opportunities may become inevitable, undermining economic growth and social equity.99 In this 
context, frontier education has become a key determinant of socio-economic mobility: individuals 
equipped with adaptive, future-ready skills are more likely to thrive, whereas those without such skills 
risk being left behind.100
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Enhancing STEM Education: As discussed, the current workforce must be 
equipped with the right skills to meet the demands of increasingly technology-driven job markets. In this 
context, science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) education has emerged as a foundational 
pillar for individual career readiness and a critical determinant of national economic development.
STEM Education as a Strategic Lever for National Competitiveness and Growth
At the tertiary level, STEM education is essential in preparing graduates to meet the immediate 
demands of the job market and engage with frontier technologies such as AI, quantum computing, 
biotechnology, and advanced data analytics. These rapidly evolving domains require more than 
99	  Australian Government. (2023). STEM Skills Are Crucial for Australia’s Changing Future. Why is STEM important? Department of 
Education. 26 October 2023. Retrieved 12 May 2025, from https://www.education.gov.au/australian-curriculum/.
100	 The term frontier education is not yet a widely adopted or standardized concept. It refers to “educational approaches that enable 
students to cross the boundaries, re-define presuppositions and create a fertile ground for providing original answers to current 
problems and challenges ahead.” (See L. Nyka & E. Marczak (2023) “Frontier education for a sustainable future-speculative design 
in architecture as a transdisciplinary experiment,” Global Journal of Engineering Education, v.25, no.1, 6). An essence of frontier 
education lies in fostering new insights by dismantling traditional disciplinary silos and promoting collaboration across diverse fields.


===== PAGE 92 =====

76 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
technical proficiency; they demand interdisciplinary thinking, ethical reasoning, and adaptability, all of 
which higher education institutions must strategically develop. In this light, tertiary STEM graduates 
are not merely participants in the economy; they are the driving forces behind it, powering innovation, 
productivity, competitiveness, and long-term growth, representing strategic national investments in 
the future.101
The emphasis on STEM education in Indonesia is a relatively recent development, which 
gained momentum around 2014.102 Since then, the country has made significant progress. The 
proportion of STEM graduates rose from just 17.3% in 2015 to approximately 30% by 2022 
(Figure 9[a]). This upward trend reflects the GOI’s strong commitment to advancing STEM education 
and sustaining its momentum into the future; however, challenges to STEM education persist, 
including low foundational competencies, limited teacher capacity, urban–rural disparities, and weak 
industry–education linkages. For example, according to the 2022 PISA survey (Programme for 
International Student Assessment), Indonesian students scored below the OECD average in mathematics, 
reading, and science.103
The performance gap among Indonesian students remains striking: about 82% of students scored below 
the OECD average in mathematics (32%), whereas only 9% qualified as top performers. In reading 
and science, approximately 75% and 66% of students, respectively, fell below the OECD average; 
however, around 7% achieved top-performer status in each subject.104 This broad gap suggests that while 
a small cohort of students demonstrates high potential, most struggle to meet basic proficiency levels, 
highlighting deep-rooted disparities in the education system.
These findings underscore the urgent need to embed STEM education in formal and 
vocational education as early as possible. Early exposure to integrated STEM learning can help 
build foundational competencies, thereby reducing the gap that often widens at the secondary and 
tertiary levels.

STEM Education as a Cross-Sectoral Policy Imperative
As discussed, other structural impediments, such as weak linkages between education and industry, 
further complicate the STEM education landscape. These challenges reveal that STEM education is 
a multifaceted agenda as well as a multi-dimensional and cross-sectoral concern that intersects with 
workforce development, innovation policy, and social equity.
Furthermore, STEM education constitutes an inter-ministerial policy domain, requiring coordinated 
action across several line ministries, including the Ministry of Education, Ministry of Industry, Ministry 
of Research and Technology, and other relevant agencies. Given the strong correlation that exists 
between STEM education and national development, all stakeholders within the GOI must align their 
efforts to address these systemic barriers.
It is immensely encouraging that the GOI has demonstrated a firm commitment to advancing STEM 
education; however, to translate this commitment into measurable progress, the existing impediments 
must be addressed with urgency and coherence in policy.
Key Actors and Stakeholders
The active engagement of key stakeholders is essential to overcoming these challenges and ensuring the 
long-term success of STEM education initiatives.

conducting pedagogical research, and preparing the next generation of STEM educators.
