Moreover, the engagement of international development partners, including UNESCO, the Asian 
Development Bank (ADB), and the World Bank, will be critical. These organizations can provide 
technical assistance, funding support, and access to global best practices, thereby strengthening the 
initiative’s design and implementation.
(Strategic Thrust) Accelerating High-Growth Industries and Revitalizing Low-Productivity 
Sectors
As discussed, Indonesia is undergoing a profound structural transformation in its economic 
fabric. Currently, traditional sectors with deep historical significance, such as agriculture, 
forestry, and fishing, contribute a significantly smaller share to national output than it did in 
previous decades. Meanwhile, the service sector has quietly but steadily emerged as a key driver 
of economic growth and employment opportunitfries, signaling a broader shift toward a 
knowledge- and innovation-driven economy; however, a pressing concern persists. The manufacturing 
sector, long regarded as the engine of Indonesia’s economic ascent, is showing signs of stagnation, if 
not outright decline. Recent contractions in output, declining competitiveness, and widespread factory 
closures have raised concerns about premature deindustrialization, intensifying concerns about the 
sector’s long-term viability.105
In this context, the GOI must proactively respond to these evolving dynamics. Responses include 
reevaluating economic and industrial policies, strengthening value chains, and investing in innovation 
and workforce upskilling to ensure that manufacturing remains a pillar of national productivity and 
sustainable growth.
The RPJMN 2025–2029 explicitly recognizes this structural transition and calls for a strategic 
reallocation of national resources to enhance productivity, competitiveness, and economic resilience. 
Approaches include prioritizing high-value-added industries, accelerating digital transformation, and 
strengthening human capital to support emerging sectors. By doing so, Indonesia aims to achieve 
its near-term growth imperative, reaching 8% economic growth by 2029, as outlined in the national 
development strategy.106
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Nurturing High-Impact and Emerging Industries and Revitalizing Legacy 
Sectors: This section is based on the recognition that TFP is the primary driver of GDP growth in 
Indonesia, as emphasized throughout the chapters. A consistent association has been ascertained between 
the growth of TFP and the expansion of GDP, supporting the idea that improving TFP is a critical 
pathway to accelerate national growth. Consequently, identifying industrial sectors with the greatest 
potential to initiate, facilitate, and accelerate technological progress and innovation is imperative for 
advancing TFP and strengthening overall economic performance.
As presented in the previous chapters, 17 major industrial sectors and 24 manufacturing industries, 
classified under the 2022 Indonesian Standard Industrial Classification (KLBI), were assessed with the 
following determinants:
	•
Share of contribution to GDP;
	•
Sectoral/industrial growth rates;
	•
Employment share;
	•
Employment growth;
	•
Export share;
105	 Firman, M. (2025). Factories struggle as Indonesia faces industrial challenges in 2025,” 05 March 2025. Asia Pacific solidarity Network.

The analysis covers the five-year period from 2018 to 2022, with export-related indicators evaluated 
over a three-year period based on data availability.
Based on this assessment, this study identifies and clusters ten high-impact industrial sectors with the 
most significant potential to catalyze technological upgrading, enhance competitiveness, and drive 
productivity-led growth.
In parallel, it also highlights ten legacy sectors, industries that have historically been significant 
contributors to national development but are now characterized by sluggish growth and declining 
competitiveness and therefore warrant targeted revitalization.
High-Performing and Emerging Industries for National Productivity and Innovation
To unlock sustainable growth and boost national growth, strategic emphasis must shift toward industries 
that act as productivity multipliers, i.e., those that amplify gains across the economy as a whole 
through innovation, digital transformation, and investment in human capital. These sectors underpin 
technological progress and shape the infrastructure, services, and capabilities essential for promoting 
future-ready development.
Policy directions within this domain should prioritize:
	•
Digital transformation and the adoption of AI to enhance productivity and economic dynamism;
	•
Logistics and infrastructure efficiency to ease structural cost burdens;
	•
Improving human capital and well-being to establish foundations for TFP growth.
The industries identified in Table 7 represent key sectors that the GOI is recommended to nurture. 
Accordingly, public policy interventions, strategic directions, and specific policy focus areas designed 
to advance systemic productivity across the economy are also outlined in the same table.

Industrial Sector Renewal for Inclusive Growth and Enterprise Resilience
A set of targeted sectors has been identified for targeted interventions (Table 8) to sustain inclusive economic 
transformation and prepare key industries for the demands of a more competitive and resilient economy. 
Many of these industries once served as dynamic engines of growth; now, they struggle with sluggish

Policy directions emphasize three interlinked imperatives:
	•
Business rationalization to revitalize outdated structures and streamline fragmented operations;
	•
Cooperative strengthening to revitalize collective capacity and modernize traditional enterprise 
models; and
	•
Environmental-technological upgrading to embed sustainability and prepare industries for future relevance.
These sectors, often comprising MSMEs, face persistent structural constraints, including informality,

formalization efforts, technical assistance, and cooperative platforms will serve as a vital means to 
unlock their potential and reposition these sectors as contributors to national resilience.

Bappenas will assume the leading role in partnership with the Ministry of Industry to realize this initiative.

specific industrial policies.

development of high-impact industries. The Ministry of Manpower will lead workforce development 
strategies by promoting vocational training, aligning industry and education, and implementing 
reskilling programs tailored to growing and declining sectors. These strategies will ensure that labor 
market transitions are inclusive, adaptive, and productivity-driven.
The Ministry of State-Owned Enterprises will leverage state-owned enterprises as transformation agents, 
catalyzing technological upgrading, investing in infrastructure and strategic value chains, and driving 
innovation, particularly in sectors aligned with national industrial priorities. BRIN will be central in 
advancing R&D, supporting innovation clusters, and facilitating knowledge transfer between scientific 
institutions and industry, particularly in high-impact sectors that leverage digital and green technologies.
The KADIN (Chamber of Commerce and Industry) will serve as the anchor platform for private sector 
engagement. It will facilitate dialogue, investment matchmaking, and capacity-building across industry 
segments, thereby contributing to competitiveness-enhancing reforms. Meanwhile, universities will 
serve as pipelines for skilled human capital, conducting applied research, and strengthening collaboration 
with industries for innovation, incubation, and sector-specific curriculum reform, particularly critical to 
the development of high-tech and digital sectors.
International Development Partners will provide technical assistance, pilot financing for reform and 
restructuring, policy benchmarking, and capacity-building to accelerate the design and execution of industrial 
transformation, especially in green technology, regulatory reform, and inclusive value chain development.
(Strategic Enabler/Initiator) Stimulating Business Creation and Unlocking Underutilized Industrial 
Potential: As highlighted in the previous chapter, Indonesia’s population exceeds 280 million, with 
approximately 68% of the population in the working-age group.107 This demographic profile highlights 
the pressing need for a dynamic base of enterprises to generate employment and diversify income sources.

82 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Recent socio-economic data also reveal that Indonesia’s middle class population has been shrinking, 
particularly in the aftermath of the COVID-19 pandemic.108 At the same time, the youth unemployment 
rate hovers around 13%, significantly higher than the national average of approximately 3.3%.109 These 
developments underscore a pressing need for the creation of new business ventures, particularly those 
capable of absorbing unemployed youth and revitalizing economic momentum across regions, now and 
in the near future. In this context, the creation and nurturing of new enterprises are not merely desirable; 
they are imperative.
University Start-ups as Engines of Transformation
Indonesia’s economic transformation increasingly hinges on its ability to cultivate a vibrant 
entrepreneurial ecosystem, one that supports traditional MSMEs and accelerates the emergence of 
high-growth, innovation-driven firms, as discussed in the preceding section. University-based start-
ups are among the most promising engines of this transformation, positioned at the intersection of 
research, talent, and technology. Such ventures are uniquely positioned to commercialize academic 
innovation, respond to local development challenges, and contribute to the diversification of Indonesia’s 
industrial base.
Indonesia’s universities are increasingly entering the national innovation landscape, helping to nurture 
a new generation of entrepreneurs. Although explicit figures are not yet available, a growing number 
of Indonesian universities are currently establishing technology transfer offices (TTOs) or startup 
incubation units.
It is encouraging that university-linked start-ups are emerging in diverse business domains such as 
agritech, edtech, healthtech, and the creative industries, particularly in regional hubs such as Bandung, 
Yogyakarta, and Surabaya.110,111 These efforts to promote and nurture the startup ecosystem are reflected 
in the 2025 Global Startup Ecosystem Index, where Indonesia ranks 45th globally, just behind Malaysia 
(44th), affirming its growing leadership in the Southeast Asian innovation landscape.112 However, 
Singapore remains the regional benchmark, currently ranked fourth globally, behind only the US, the UK, 
and Israel. This gap underscores the scale of ambition and investment required for Indonesia to compete in 
the global innovation landscape.
Indonesia must strengthen its innovation infrastructure by improving IP frameworks, establishing 
seed funding mechanisms, fostering mentorship networks, and enhancing industry–academia linkages 
to fully unlock the potential of university start-ups and accelerate the development of a vibrant 
startup ecosystem.113,114
Paving a Strategic Path toward a Regenerative and Future-Ready Bioeconomy
Despite its status as one of the world’s largest economies, Indonesia maintains vast latent economic 
potential across several underutilized industrial sectors. Among the most striking examples is the marine-
based Blue Economy. As the world’s largest archipelagic nation, encompassing over 17,000 islands and 
an expansive maritime territory, Indonesia holds immense promise in sectors such as sustainable fisheries, 
ocean-based renewable energy, marine biotechnology, and coastal tourism. However, paradoxically, 
these opportunities remain largely underexploited; therefore, the GOI has recently formulated a national

roadmap for developing the Blue Economy, aiming to unlock new sources of growth while ensuring 
marine sustainability and sovereignty.
Despite its comparable potential, Indonesia’s bioindustry sector has remained largely outside the 
focus of national development policy. It is constrained by a historical pattern of resource exploitation 
rather than guided by innovation and balanced development.115 Indonesia has one of the world’s largest 
tropical forest reserves, which offers a treasure trove of biomass, genetic resources, and ecological 
services. However, the country has historically viewed its forests primarily through an extractive lens.116 
Instead of being harnessed as strategic national assets for sustainable innovation in biopharmaceuticals, 
bioenergy, bioplastics, and green agriculture, these ecosystems have often been exploited in a short-
term manner. Unlocking the long-term value of Indonesia’s bioeconomy will require a paradigm shift to 
reposition biodiversity as a foundation for resilient, high-value, and inclusive economic transformation.
The evolving technological landscape and the worldwide calls for green and inclusive growth may 
present an opportunity for Indonesia to develop a comprehensive national roadmap for bioindustry 
development. Such a roadmap could consolidate existing expertise and policy wisdom into a unified 
framework for harnessing sustainable, renewable, and innovation-driven bio-assets as engines of 
national development. Unlocking the long-term value of Indonesia’s bioeconomy can transform 
latent natural capital into tangible opportunities, spurring job creation, industrial diversification, and 
rural revitalization.
Enabling technologies have matured, making downstream bioindustry value chain development 
increasingly feasible; however, this transition faces various obstacles. First among them is the challenge 
arising from the fragmented policy architecture, marked by overlapping mandates across ministries 
and the absence of a unified national bioeconomy strategy.117 Compounding this issue is a persistent 
extractivist development paradigm; forests and biodiversity are viewed primarily as export commodities, 
not as renewable platforms for innovation and inclusive growth.118
Moreover, legal and institutional deficiencies in IP protection, particularly in areas such as bioprospecting 
and traditional knowledge, hinder incentives for investment in high-value bioproducts. The broader bio-
based innovation ecosystem also remains underdeveloped, lacking sufficient funding, infrastructure, 
and human capital pipelines. Emerging bio-entrepreneurs often face restricted access to certification 
schemes, sustainable supply chains, and green market channels. At the same time, early-stage financing 
remains scarce and risk-averse.
Furthermore, land tenure insecurity and weak community rights continue to deter long-term 
investment, particularly in forest-based innovation models that depend on ecological stewardship and 
local participation.
These challenges underscore that Indonesia’s bioeconomy transition relies on mobilizing its natural 
potential and cultivating an ecosystem characterized by coordinated policies, robust knowledge systems, 
and equitable governance frameworks.
In sum, a comprehensive bioindustry roadmap, anchored in inter-ministerial coordination, could 
consolidate fragmented mandates, overcome extractivist legacies, and harness biodiversity as a catalyst 
for a high-value, innovation-driven sector.
Key actors and Stakeholders
Realizing the transformative potential of university-based start-ups within Indonesia’s broader 
innovation landscape requires a well-orchestrated ecosystem of aligned and proactive stakeholders.

In partnership with the Ministry of Environment and Forestry and BRIN, Bappenas will lead this 
initiative by formulating the Bioeconomy Development Roadmap and integrating it into the broader 
restructuring of bioindustry development planning under the RPJMN 2025–39. BRIN will be responsible 
for bio-based R&D, innovation funding, and technology transfer, anchoring the plan for establishing 
national bio-innovation hubs. Meanwhile, as custodian of Indonesia’s forest resources, the Ministry 
of Environment and Forestry will formulate feasible strategies for land tenure reform, biodiversity 
conservation, and the development of sustainable forest-based sub-industries.

bioindustry development. The Ministry of Industry will assess the feasibility of industrial  scaling, 
certification systems, and downstream bio-based manufacturing. The Ministry of Finance is expected to 
explore and mobilize green fiscal instruments and financing facilities to support this transition.
Private sector participation will be critical to the success of this initiative. Universities will contribute 
through research and innovation, while the Chamber of Commerce and Industry (an active proponent of 
the bioeconomy) will collaborate with Bappenas in advocacy, standard setting, and facilitating public–
private dialogues. Community enterprises will serve as key advisors in promoting inclusive value chain 
participation and strengthening local stewardship.

International development partners (such as the UNDP, UNEP, FAO, GIZ, JICA, and KOICA) are

align with the overarching goals of promoting the green economy and biodiversity conservation.
(Strategic Thrust) Closing the Gap between Large Enterprises (LE) and Small and Medium 
Enterprises (SME)
Indonesia’s economy is driven by over 56 million MSMEs; thus, its development trajectory relies on 
their survival and integration into higher-value production ecosystems.119 The GOI has introduced 
a wide range of support programs, including financing schemes, digital adoption initiatives, and 
entrepreneurship development; however, the scale of these efforts is minuscule compared to the sheer 
number, diversity, and geographical dispersion of SMEs across the archipelago. Accordingly, bridging 
the capability gap between LEs and SMEs is a strategic priority, not merely for reasons of inclusivity 
but as a necessary condition for global competitiveness.
Bridging the Divide and Forging Synergy
The strategic transformation of Indonesia’s SME landscape cannot rely solely on public programs. In a 
nation of over 56 million MSMEs, tasks such as capacity enhancement must cascade through the connective 
tissue of productive partnerships, particularly those rooted in long-term collaboration with established LEs. 
The GOI has launched commendable interventions, including the Kredit Usaha Rakyat (KUR) scheme, 
digital acceleration platforms such as Startup4Industry, and the PROMISE II Impact program; however, 
these initiatives face persistent challenges in scale, precision targeting, and sustainability.120, 121, 122
Despite the GOI’s efforts, many SMEs in Indonesia remain outside the formal support ecosystem due 
to systemic barriers, including limited awareness, administrative complexity, and restrictive eligibility 
criteria. For example, the large number of SMEs operating informally or without registration further 
complicates outreach and program targeting.123
Moreover, a persistent mismatch exists between the design of public programs and the actual needs of 
SMEs. Government initiatives often prioritize financial access and regulatory compliance; however, they 
frequently fall short in delivering capability building, technology transfer, and market integration that SMEs 
urgently require to thrive in today’s technology- and innovation-driven economy. This gap is particularly 
notable in Indonesia, where SME participation in global and regional value chains remains relatively limited.
A Strategic Realignment of Leveraging LE–SME Partnerships
Against the systemic constraints and mismatches between programs and actual needs, LE–
SME partnerships emerge as catalytic and practical complements to the development of industry 
competitiveness. Unlike public initiatives, well-structured partnerships with LEs offer SMEs embedded 
access to key drivers of long-term competitiveness. Such partnerships can provide quality assurance 
systems, modern production processes, market insights, exposure to global standards, and, more 
importantly, access to the global market.
LEs serve as SME capability escalators, transferring technology as well as operational know-how and 
access to global marketplaces, helping bridge the divide between Indonesia’s SME majority and the 
competitive imperatives of a digital, export-focused economy. The benefits to LEs are also substantial,

NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
as these partnerships can forge robust supply chains, strengthening the ecosystem and enhancing 
economic inclusion.
Bottlenecks in Indonesia’s LE–SME Partnerships
Despite growing needs, LE–SME partnerships in Indonesia remain underleveraged and underutilized 
due to several bottlenecks. These are primarily attributable to (1) legal constraints stemming from the 
current regulatory framework, as outlined in Law No. 20/2008. The law imposes significant compliance 
burdens on LEs while offering limited clarity and enforceability regarding SME obligations, which 
ultimately discourages LEs from engaging in long-term partnerships.124 Additionally, (2) institutional 
fragmentation can hinder such partnerships, as public support remains dispersed across over 40 
government agencies with overlapping mandates. This fragmentation leads to cumbersome coordination, 
thereby undermining strategic alignment.125,126
Furthermore, SMEs face resource constraints, such as limited technological capacity, low workforce 
education levels, and minimal or no R&D activity, further contributing to challenges. The structural 
constraints inherent in SMEs erode their capacity and competitiveness. Even in the presence of linkages, 
digital disconnects, delayed payments, and other factors erode SME confidence and inhibit long-term 
collaboration. Consequently, Indonesia has yet to fully realize the productivity gains, export potential, 
and inclusive innovation benefits that more structured and equitable LE–SME partnerships could deliver.
To unlock the full potential of LE–SME partnership, the GOI must transition from fragmented 
interventions toward a more institutionalized, incentivized, and innovation-driven partnership model. 
This shift should prioritize strengthening the legal and regulatory framework—particularly Law No. 
20/2008—to ensure fair and reciprocal agreements between LEs and SMEs.
In line with this approach, the GOI should advance key policy initiatives during the RPJMN 
2025–29, such as the Facilitation of Partnerships and Market Access. This initiative supports collaboration 
between micro and small enterprises (UMK) and larger business entities (UMB), in accordance with the 
mandate of Government Regulation No. 7 of 2021.
Another strategic measure under the RPJMN 2025–29 is the development of the Affirmative and 
Productive Business Card, aimed at aligning and harmonizing currently fragmented government 
interventions across various ministries and agencies. By establishing gradual and coordinated MSME 
development standards, these efforts seek to strengthen the ecosystem for sustainable LE–SME 
partnerships, expand market access, and enhance the competitiveness of Indonesian SMEs.
While these initiatives offer a clear policy direction, substantial efforts are still required to ensure effective 
implementation. This includes operationalizing programs, fostering inter-ministerial coordination, building 
institutional capacity, and establishing robust monitoring mechanisms to achieve the desired impact.
Additionally, the GOI should promote the creation of a shared digital procurement platform accessible 
to both LEs and SMEs. Such a platform would enable LEs to aggregate and integrate SME products 
into global value chains, thereby contributing to a more competitive and resilient production ecosystem 
in Indonesia.
Key Actors and Stakeholders
The active engagement and commitment of the following actors/stakeholders are essential to strengthen 
and sustain long-term LE–SME partnerships.

KADIN, Kamar Dagang dan Industri Indonesia (Chamber of Commerce and Industry, Indonesia); LEs, large enterprises; SMEs, small and 
medium enterprises
The active leadership of Bappenas is essential (working in coordination with multiple government 
stakeholders) to strengthen long-term LE–SME partnerships. In particular, collaboration with the 
Ministry of Law and Human Rights is important to ensure that the revision of Law No. 20/2008 is 
legally sound, harmonized with existing statutes, and aligned with constitutional principles.
The Ministry of Industry, Ministry of MSMEs, and Ministry of Trade will also play key roles, overseeing 
sectoral policy design and implementation related to value chain integration and SME development. 
Furthermore, strong and sustained engagement from the House of Representatives will be vital to 
scrutinize and pass legislative amendments as well as oversee implementation and allocate budgetary 
resources that enable meaningful LE–SME collaboration.
At the same time, the Ministry of Communication and Informatics will assume a pivotal role in this initiative, 
providing strategic guidance for the development and management of a national digital procurement system 
accessible to LEs and SMEs. This support will also help reduce the existing digital divide between LEs and SMEs.
It is also critical to muster the active participation of LEs, SMEs, universities, and business associations (such 
as KADIN) at every stage of the implementation of the initiative. Their engagement is essential to ensure 
that the system reflects real-world needs, fosters confidence, and supports long-term partnerships. Moreover, 
international development partners are expected to contribute through funding, technical assistance, and the 
sharing of global best practices, strengthening implementation and long-term sustainability.
Toward a Smart People, Smart Economy, and Smart Nation: A Strategic 
Roadmap to Uplifting National Productivity
To translate strategic intent into practical action, this master plan presents a phased implementation 
roadmap spanning the period from 2025 to 2029. Aligned with Indonesia’s national development priorities 
and grounded in the core values embedded in successive development plans, the roadmap outlines the 
phasing of five interlinked strategic pillars. Each pillar is supported by thrusts and enabling actions aimed at 
enhancing productivity, bridging capability gaps, and strengthening national competitiveness and resilience.
The roadmap begins with the baseline and assessment phase, dedicating the first half of 2025 to 
comprehensive cross-pillar assessments (Figure 10 Figure 1). This phase will focus on establishing 
a baseline for identifying key constraints, institutional preparedness, and expected collaborations, 
ensuring that the GOI’s interventions align with real-world challenges.


===== PAGE 104 =====

88 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
NEW FRAMEWORK FOR GROWTH AND PRODUCTIVITY 
Building on this groundwork, the acceleration and integration phase focuses on executing high-
impact, scalable initiatives across infrastructure, education, R&D, and enterprise ecosystems. Strategic 
investments can bridge digital and technological divides, operationalize value chain linkages between 
LEs and MSMEs, and retool the workforce for emerging, future-focused sectors.
The final scaling and sustainability phase ensures that productivity gains will be sustained and 
institutionalized. By 2029, the strategic focus must integrate TFP metrics into national and regional 
planning systems, expand successful pilot initiatives, and mitigate structural risks that may erode progress.
The full roadmap is presented as a Gantt-style timeline in Appendix 2, visually linking each pillar, 
thrust, and enabler to specific milestones. These milestones represent progress markers, serving as 
mechanisms for course correction and supporting adaptive governance, inter-agency coordination, and 
performance monitoring.
This phased trajectory outlines a pragmatic yet ambitious pathway toward realizing a “Smart People, 
Smart Economy, and Smart Nation,” where productivity is no longer treated as a narrow indicator; it is 
instead embraced as the core architecture of inclusive and sustainable growth.


===== PAGE 105 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 89
KEY FINDINGS, CONCLUSIONS, AND 
STRATEGIC PATH FORWARD
This master plan study adopts a hybrid research approach, integrating rigorous data analysis with 
a normative policy framework. These empirical and normative components form the structural 
foundation of this study, which aims to chart a national pathway toward enhancing productivity and 
long-term competitiveness.
This study is anchored in a model that highlights the mutually reinforcing relationship among three 
dynamic forces that shape a nation’s productive potential: economic growth, industrial development, 
and productivity.
The central question embedded within this master plan is: How can Indonesia achieve and sustain the 
growth necessary to transition from a middle-income to a high-income economy by 2045? To address 
this fundamental inquiry, this study examines the feasibility of achieving the 6%–8% annual growth rate 
deemed critical to realizing the goals outlined in Visi Indonesia Emas 2045.
This research calls for a deeper understanding of the structural and productivity-related constraints 
across sectors and regions. Accordingly, this study poses the following inquiries into the interrelated 
nexus of growth, productivity, and industrial development:
	•
What have been the sources of Indonesia’s economic growth?
	•
What is the current state of productivity across Indonesia’s industries?
	•
How does productivity vary across regions and industry types?
	•
How have these dynamics shaped national productivity trends and influenced economic expansion?
Grounded in data and foresight, this master plan presents a forward-looking, evidence-based framework 
for enhancing national productivity and driving transformation.
Uncovering the Growth-Industrial Development-Productivity Nexus: 
Empirical Evidence and Insights
Findings on Indonesia’s Growth Dynamics
Indonesia’s growth trajectory reveals critical structural features and emerging vulnerabilities. Based on 
a comprehensive empirical analysis, the following findings outline key characteristics of the nation’s 
economic expansion and the shifting composition of its growth drivers.
1.	 Growth reliant on domestic consumption: Indonesia’s sustained economic expansion from 2004 
to 2023 was heavily consumption- and investment-driven, with exports playing a marginal and 
diminishing role, particularly in comparison to its ASEAN peers. This situation signals potential 
vulnerability to external shocks and limited exposure to the benefits of global trade.


===== PAGE 106 =====

90 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
KEY FINDINGS, CONCLUSIONS, AND STRATEGIC PATH FORWARD
2.	 Weakening of the industrial backbone: Beyond the service sector, industrial output has declined 
across the board, particularly in manufacturing, which has historically been the bedrock of economic 
transformation. This situation suggests a departure from the classic industrialization pathway.
3.	 Signs of premature deindustrialization: The shift in the labor force from manufacturing to 
services, combined with a stagnant manufacturing base, has amplified concerns about premature 
deindustrialization, raising questions about the long-term structural integrity of Indonesia’s growth.
4.	 Structural inequality across regions: A pronounced output gap among provinces, driven by unequal 
access to the factors of production, highlights persistent regional imbalances that risk entrenching 
socio-economic disparities if left unaddressed.
5.	 Enterprise capability divide: Indonesia’s industrial ecosystem reveals a deep chasm between LEs 
and micro/small enterprises, indicating that inclusive productivity and innovation remain elusive.
Findings on Industrial Development and Growth
Indonesia’s long-term growth prospects are closely tied to the structure and performance of its 
industrial sectors. This part of the report synthesizes key diagnostic findings on how patterns in sectoral 
development, workforce dynamics, export composition, and regional and firm size disparities shape the 
outcomes of overall growth and economic transformation.
1.	 Narrow sectoral base of growth: Growth has been concentrated in a few sectors such as 
manufacturing, agriculture, wholesale/retail trade, and mining, with recent momentum from ICT, 
finance, and human health and social work activities. Nonetheless, the limited contribution from 
other industrial segments raises concerns about the sustainability of growth.
2.	 Structural workforce shifts to services: Despite absorbing a large portion of the workforce, 
agriculture and manufacturing demonstrate sluggish employment growth. In contrast, services (e.g., 
transport, business activities, healthcare) are expanding rapidly, signaling an occurrence of sectoral 
labor migration.
3.	 Shallow export structure: Manufactured exports remain dominated by low value added and primary 
or intermediate products, exposing the economy to external risks. Indonesia’s share of medium- and 
high-tech exports is low compared to its ASEAN peers, underscoring the urgent need for industrial 
upgrading and diversification.
4.	 Uneven regional growth: Java and Sumatra dominate the national economy, whereas peripheral 
regions (e.g., Sulawesi and Maluku) demonstrate growing potential, particularly where 
manufacturing-led growth is emerging. However, agriculture still dominates the national economic 
landscape, pointing to ongoing challenges in regional industrial transformation.
5.	 Emergence of new growth sectors: Sectors such as information and communication and human 
health and social work are proliferating, driven by digitization, demographic shifts, and environmental 
transitions. These sectors offer promise but require targeted policy and skill investment.
6.	 Structural barriers in manufacturing: Indonesia’s manufacturing sector remains highly 
fragmented, with small-scale firms comprising the bulk of the base. Their limited productivity, 
scalability, and technological capacity highlight the structural barriers to industrial deepening.
Findings on Productivity-Growth Nexus
Indonesia’s productivity performance reveals a complex and uneven growth landscape. While TFP 
is emerging as the key driver of long-term competitiveness, the country continues to face persistent 
structural barriers, particularly in manufacturing, regional equity, and enterprise capability, thereby 
hindering more profound industrial transformation, productivity enhancement, and growth. The 
following findings highlight seven critical patterns shaping Indonesia’s productivity-growth nexus.


===== PAGE 107 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 91
KEY FINDINGS, CONCLUSIONS, AND STRATEGIC PATH FORWARD
1.	 TFP as a game changer: Sustainable growth in Indonesia depends less on labor and capital and 
more on productivity gains.
2.	 Reinvigorating manufacturing: The stagnation in productivity of this traditional engine signals a 
need for strategic renewal.
3.	 Growing service sector: Emerging service sectors are promising; however, their impact is yet to be 
fully realized.
4.	 Persistent regional disparities: Stark interregional gaps in productivity and output demand require 
geographically targeted strategies.
5.	 Micro and small enterprises under strain: The majority of firms experience negative TFP growth, 
constraining inclusive economic development.
6.	 Intangibles matter: Capital inputs alone do not drive productivity; innovation, skills, and institutions 
play pivotal roles.
7.	 Lagging behind global leaders: Despite outperforming regional peers, such as Malaysia, Thailand, 
and Vietnam, Indonesia’s productivity levels remain far below those of advanced economies.
Summary, Insights, and Strategic Implications
The central insight from the analytical assessment of Indonesia’s growth–productivity–industrial 
development nexus is clear: TFP plays a defining role in shaping long-term economic growth. Labor and 
capital remain essential inputs; however, their marginal contributions are diminishing in importance. 
Therefore, Indonesia’s historical reliance on factor accumulation can no longer sustain the trajectory 
required to achieve advanced economy status.
The analysis reveals that movements in TFP account for a significant share of output variation, 
underscoring its importance as the core engine of future expansion. In this light, the GOI must 
place TFP enhancement at the center of its national development strategy. This insight implies a 
fundamental shift in Indonesia’s development paradigm, from a factor-driven model to one anchored in 
TFP-led growth.
Importantly, improving TFP is not a singular intervention; it is the result of a multi-dimensional 
investment ecosystem. Technological achievements and advancements, quality education, robust R&D, 
modern, resilient infrastructure, and capable institutions all serve as critical levers.
These enablers must not be seen as fiscal burdens but as foundational investments in Indonesia’s long-
term productive potential; neglecting them risks reinforcing structural stagnation. In contrast, sustained 
and strategic investment in innovation systems, skills development, digital and physical connectivity, 
and industrial upgrading can catalyze productivity gains across regions, sectors, and smaller enterprises. 
This approach enables TFP to transform from an abstract residual into a tangible, policy-driven force for 
enhancing national competitiveness and achieving inclusive, long-term prosperity.
Strategic Levers to Unlock Growth Potential and Accelerate Productivity
Building on the insights from the preceding section, this section identifies key strategic directions to 
catalyze TFP growth and unlock Indonesia’s broader productivity potential. To this end, the GOI is 
encouraged to initiate the following five priority areas.
1.	 Building national consensus: Promote productivity awareness and community-led initiatives to 
curb productivity losses (including postharvest waste) and reduce systemic inefficiencies.


===== PAGE 108 =====

92 | INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029
KEY FINDINGS, CONCLUSIONS, AND STRATEGIC PATH FORWARD
2.	 Reorienting development toward TFP-led growth: Ensure regulatory coherence through 
institutional mechanisms that support innovation and long-term investment.
3.	 Modernizing infrastructure and advancing digital transformation: Expand smart infrastructure, 
intelligent logistics, and regional tech parks to bridge productivity gaps.
4.	 Strengthening education and research linkages: Align R&D and education systems with future-
ready competencies and greater commercial relevance.
5.	 Diversifying growth drivers and bridging the enterprise gap: Accelerate sectoral upgrading and 
foster stronger linkages between LEs and MSMEs to spread productivity gains.
Strategic Roadmap Overview
To translate these strategic levers into action, a phased roadmap (aligned with the 2025–2029 planning 
cycle) outlines the sequencing of the five key pillars and their enabling initiatives (Appendix 1). The 
roadmap comprises three phases: (1) baseline and assessment, (2) acceleration and integration over 
time, and (3) scaling and sustainability focused on securing payoffs and mitigating downside risks 
(Figure 1). A detailed roadmap is presented in Appendix 2.
FIGURE 1

Nonetheless, the implementation must extend beyond strategy design. Indonesia possesses many 
productivity-oriented policies; however, institutional overlaps, policy inconsistencies, and limited 
cross-agency coordination reduce their impact. For these strategies to deliver meaningful policy 
outcomes, focused and sustained whole-of-government alignment is essential. This master plan’s five-
year horizon presents a crucial window of opportunity to act, adjust, and embed a productivity-focused 
development trajectory.
TFP must become the center of Indonesia’s national growth strategy, not as an abstract notion but as 
the outcome of coordinated investments in technology, education, R&D, infrastructure, and institutions. 
They should not be perceived as costs but investments for the future. Only a continued commitment to 
these enablers can help Indonesia bridge its productivity gap and make inclusive, high-income growth 
possible by 2045.
The Way Forward
As discussed in previous chapters, this master plan spans a five-year horizon, calibrated to account 
for Indonesia’s rapidly evolving development landscape. Therefore, the proposed strategies should be 
subject to comprehensive evaluation at the end of the period, with necessary adjustments made to reflect 
emerging challenges, opportunities, and shifts in the external environment.


===== PAGE 109 =====

INDONESIA’S NATIONAL PRODUCTIVITY MASTER PLAN 2025-2029 | 93
KEY FINDINGS, CONCLUSIONS, AND STRATEGIC PATH FORWARD
Crucially, this study’s findings highlight that Indonesia is not constrained by a lack of policies but 
challenges in ensuring policy consistency, reducing institutional overlap, and overcoming fragmented 
implementation. Gaps in coordination among public institutions further compound this complexity.
These systemic issues call for focused and continued attention from the GOI to translate strategy into 
sustainable and meaningful impact. It is essential to strengthen institutional coherence by clarifying 
roles and mandates and fostering whole-of-government alignment to advance the productivity agenda in 
an accountable, adaptive, and future-ready manner.

APPENDIX
Appendix 1. Vision, Mission, and Strategic Pillars
Vision
Smart People, Smart Economy, Smart Nation
Mission
Empower a highly skilled, innovation-driven workforce, strengthen a competitive economy 
through R&D excellence, and advance toward a forward-thinking nation at the forefront of 
technology
Strategic Pillars
Strategic Thrusts
Strategic Enablers/Initiators
1. Mustering 
National 
Consensus
1.1. Enhancing productivity 
awareness by 
advocating the national 
movement of the 
8-4-2-2 Movement
1.2. Reducing Productivity 
Loss
1.1.1. Disseminating the significance of productivity 
enhancement for the sustainable development of the 
national economy
1.2.1. Fostering a corps of productivity coaches and 
consultants, utilizing retirees with experience in the 
relevant fields of industry.
1.2.2. Enhancing the converting of wasted foods to organic 
fertilizer/animal feeds program to a national-level 
recycle system, as a part of an active promotion of 
circular and green economy;
1.2.3. Establishing and expanding small- and medium-scale 
rice processing centers (RPCs) in each district to 
reduce postharvest loss.
2. Transitioning 
National 
Development 
Paradigm to 
TFP-led Growth
2.1. Improving regulatory 
stability
2.1.1. Establishing a dedicated institution to address 
regulatory stability
3. Strengthening 
Infrastructure, 
Digital 
Transformation 
and 
Intelligentization
3.1. Strengthening 
forward-looking 
infrastructure
3.1.1. Enhancing smart disaster preparedness and early 
warning system;
3.1.2. Grading up existing ports to smart and intelligent 
ports;
3.1.3. Developing techno-parks across provinces to bridge 
regional disparities and the digital divide;
3.1.4. Empowering MSMEs to lead Indonesia’s smart factory 
transformation;
4. Enhancing 
Education and 
R&D
4.1. Augmenting linkage 
between R&D and 
commercialization
4.2. Enhancing future-ready 
education in an era of 
technological frontiers
4.1.1. Reorganizing the national R&D structure to distinguish 
short- and long-term priorities;
4.2.1. Enhancing STEM education
5. Diversifying and 
Developing 
Growth Drivers
5.1. Accelerating high-
growth Industries and 
revitalizing low-
productivity sectors
5.2. Bridging the gap 
between LE and MSME
5.1.1. Nurturing high impact and emerging industries, and 
revitalizing legacy sectors;
5.1.2. Stimulating business creation and unlocking 
underutilized industrial potential
5.2.1. Strengthening LE-MSME partnership;
